Library The Custody of Silence

The Custody of Silence

by Mara Els (as told to the record)

Prologue — The First Stilling

The ship that carried the founders to Cessra was called the Lachesis, and during its long dark crossing the passengers dreamed of the war they had fled.

Some of those dreams were loud. A woman in steerage woke one morning unable to speak, her jaw locked around a name she would not name for twenty years. A child of seven began to wet his bed again, though the bed-wetting had ended three years before the first bombardment. A man who had been a schoolteacher before the war refused to eat anything that had not been sealed in his own hands, and he lost half his weight before the Lachesis cleared the second jump. A pair of twins who had been inseparable since the camps began to refuse to speak to one another, because one of them had been made to do something in a yard that the other would not forgive, and the silence between them was louder than the engines.

Three of the dreamers did not wake in the usual way. They went instead to the airlock, sure that opening a door into the black was the only honest thing a person could do once they had heard what they had heard. Two of them were turned back by the watch. The third slipped past during a routine cycle, and the captain, who had been awake the whole night waiting for the slip, let the airlock cycle anyway, because the alternative had been to wake the passenger and begin the long conversation about what was owed, and what was owed was more than a captain could pay in a single watch. The captain logged the loss as equipment failure. No one corrected the log, because correcting it would have meant reading it, and reading it would have meant saying the passenger's name aloud in front of the passengers, which was itself a kind of airlock.

At the heart of the Lachesis the founders held a council, and the question before them was the question they had carried with them since the first bombardment: should they bring their memories with them, or leave them, cleanly, in the ruins they were leaving? Memory, the war had taught them, was the substance that turned a man into a thing that kills. Forgetting, on the other hand, was its own slow killing of a quieter kind, the kind that hollowed a person out from the inside and left them breathing for no reason they could name. The council sat at a long table welded to the deck, and the deck trembled under them with every breath the engines took, and they argued in voices that tried to be calm.

Oris was among the youngest of the council. She argued for keeping, but for keeping with a latch: a way to set a grief aside without losing it, returnable, so the record would hold what the mind could not. She said it as plainly as she could, and then she said it again, and then she said it a third time, because the people at the table were tired in a way that made plain words sound like new ones.

She said: a person can be entrusted with more than they can carry, if you give them a place to put down what they cannot yet bear. The place does not have to be inside them. It can be in the record, in a Keeper, in a thing the community agrees to hold. What a person cannot bear today they may be able to bear tomorrow, if tomorrow they have someone to bear it with, and if the someone does not flinch, and if the place has been kept. We must be that someone. We must build the room, and we must promise to open it when the time comes, and we must write the promise in such a way that the people who come after us can find it, even if they do not know what they are looking for.

The older founders called her soft. The ship's captain, who had no love for their politics, called her practical, because a crew that dreamed of war was a crew that might open the wrong door at the wrong hour, and the latch kept the door shut until such a time as the colony could afford to choose what to do with it. A man who had been a poet before the war said that any system that proposed to forget on behalf of a citizen was a system that proposed to lie to them, and Oris answered that the latch was not forgetting, it was holding, and the holding was the difference between a person left alone in the dark and a person whose hand was held in the dark by someone they trusted. The poet did not think this was a difference. The captain did. The council voted, by a margin of one, to keep the latch.

The latch, Oris said again, when the words were being agreed on, is not a door. A door is for the whole of a life's traffic, the comings and goings of an ordinary day. A latch is for what would unmake you on the worst night, the night no one else is in the room. A latch is small. A latch is something you can hold in one hand, and a person holding it can still sleep, and a person sleeping can still wake, and a person waking can still be received, by someone they have not yet met, in a morning they have not yet lived. It does not take the place of the wall; it sits inside the wall, and the wall is the house, and the house is for living in. What we are agreeing to is not a city of forgetting. It is a city that knows how to take care of its own worst hours, and then go back to living.

The compromise that emerged from that council was, in the end, the Stilling: a latch on the worst, never a door on the rest. It was to be used sparingly, and only for the things named in the seventh clause: the grief that would unmake a worker in their first forty days; the memory of rage that would turn a hand against another; the memory of the war itself, lest it be carried in. The clause was written in the plain hand of the settlement's first Keeper, and the words were tested, one at a time, against the objections of every founder in the room, and only the words nobody could object to were allowed to remain. It is the reason the clause is so short, and it is the reason the colony would learn, a hundred years later, that shortness is not the same as smallness.

When they landed, the dome had not fully cured.

The cold came in at the seams where the panels met, and the first winter on Cessra was colder than the founders had calculated. Inside the infirmary a man named Pellin held a child he would not hold again. The daughter had been born in the war, in a shelter that had lost its roof to a single shell, and she had come into the world already with a cough that would not leave her, and the cough had followed her across three ships and one landing and into the colony, where the dome's seal had not yet fully cured. She died in the cold, before the dome sealed, in the long grey hour that the infirmary staff would later record in the log as the third morning, and the dying had taken hours, and the hours had taken Pellin, and what was left of him at the end was a shape that breathed without being a man. The shape sat in a chair by the empty cot for the rest of the day, and the chair was where the nurse found him when the day's work had ended, and the nurse did not move him, because the nurse understood, without being told, that a man who had stopped being a man ought to be left where he was until he could remember how.

Oris went to him near dusk. She took his hands, and spoke the words the Keeper alone could speak, and the words were not a spell and not a science and not a comfort, and they were all of those things too. When she was done, Pellin looked up with dry eyes and said, quite clearly, in a voice that sounded like a man who had just woken from a long sleep in a clean bed, She is gone, then. I am glad she is not in pain. He remembered, and would always remember, that his daughter had died. He did not remember the tearing, the hours, the sound he had made, the way her small hands had gone cold at the last. He did not remember how the chair had come to be his. The settlement buried the child in the garden behind the dormitory where the weavers slept, and the colony went on working, and no one broke.

It was the first time the latch had been used in earnest on the world it was built for, and it worked.

Oris logged it. She wrote the return-clause in the margin of the record, in her plain hand, and the margin note read: held, not erased; returnable upon order of a future Assembly. Below the note she wrote sparingly, and meant it, with her whole body, in the way of a person who had seen the thing herself and did not need to be persuaded. She also wrote may be returned, and trusted a tomorrow she would not see. She chose the word returnable over recoverable on purpose, because recoverable is what you say about a thing lost by accident, and returnable is what you say about a thing set down on purpose, by a hand that meant to come back for it. The choice of the word was, in itself, a faith.

A clerk in the corner, watching the logging, noted in his own ledger, of Pellin, that he worked well. The note is the first appearance in the archive of a metric that would come to govern a century of colony life: not whether a citizen was whole, but whether they were useful. The clerk was not a cruel man. He was a tired man, in a tired hour, doing the work that was in front of him. He went home to his own supper, and his own sleep, and his own griefs, which he had been keeping for years in the only way a tired man can keep them, by not opening the door behind which they were stored.

Behind Oris, all the while, the settlement's engineer had stood and said nothing.

Her name was Tace, and she had argued, on the Lachesis and after, for a broader subtraction than the one the Assembly would adopt. She had wanted the right to set aside not only grief but the appetite for grief, the small daily habits of mourning, the customs of remembering the dead in the evenings. She had wanted, in particular, the right to forget her own children by name, because she had lost two of them to the war they had fled, and the names were a weight her back could no longer carry, and she had been honest about this, and the honesty had not helped her. She had lost the argument. The losing had schooled her into a kind of silence the colony would later formalize as peace, though no one at the time would have called it anything as grand.

The first child had been a boy of four, taken by a fever that the camps could not treat. The second had been a girl of nine, taken by a soldier's hand in a doorway, in a town whose name Tace would not say aloud even now, in the colony, where the dome held the silence in. The loss sat in her like a stone at the bottom of a well, and the well was the dome, and the dome was the colony, and the colony was the only family she had left to tend. She tended it the way she tended the looms: thoroughly, without complaint, in the hope that the tending would be enough.

The clause that became the Charter of Subtraction was, from its first breath, a compromise between those who wanted to forget everything and those who wanted to forget nothing, settled by people too tired to hold either extreme. That tiredness is the book's true origin: not malice, but fatigue. Fatigue is the most durable architect of walls, because a tired people will agree to anything that promises rest, even a rest whose terms they have not read, even a rest that asks them to be, in some quiet way, less than they were before. The founders were, in their own way, the first citizens of the wall they would die inside. They did not know this. They could not have known this. They thought they were building a house.

For a long while the rest was real. The colony's first year passed without a single citizen reliving the flight, and the looms were built on that calm, and the loom-works were the foundation, and the foundation rested on a Stilling, and the Stilling rested on a grief, and the grief was a child's death whose name was not written in the charter. Only her absence was written. The absence became the colony's first blank stone in the garden behind the weavers' dormitory. A second stone went in beside it the next winter, after a weaver named Iret lost her husband to a fault in the loom. A third stone went in the winter after that, after a boy fell from the unfinished dome and did not wake up. There were no names on the stones, because names on stones require remembering, and remembering, in the colony, required a Keeper's pen, and the pen, that first year, was only ever used to log what had been set aside, not what had been lost.

No future Assembly ever ordered the return of what Oris had set aside.

That silence, more than the stones, was the hinge.

A clause written as a bandage became, stone by stone, a wall. The keepers who added the stones told themselves, each time, that the stone was temporary. This one is different, they said, generation after generation, this one is for a grief that no one could bear, and the phrase no one could bear travelled from mouth to mouth until it sounded like a kind of law. The first Keeper could not have imagined the wall. She had written sparingly, and she had meant it, and the word sparingly is still legible in the original margin, in the archive, in her own hand. But a latch that is never opened rusts shut, and the rust was the story of the next hundred and forty years. The founders who had wanted forgetting entirely were dead within the first decade. Those who had wanted keeping had been worn down by the cold and the grief and the simple relief of a quiet mind, and the latch, left un-released, became the door it was never meant to be. By the sixtieth year, no one in the colony remembered that the latch had been meant to open. By the eightieth, no one remembered that the war had ever ended in anything but departure. By the hundredth, no one could say, with any certainty, what had been set aside or why, only that the settling of such things was the work of the Keeper, and that the work was good, and that the colony was at peace.

The captain, who had laughed when Oris spoke of trusting tomorrow, did not live to see the joke turned earnest. He died in the colony's third winter, of an illness the infirmary could not name, in the small room off the great hall that had been set aside for him because he had no family and had not wanted to trouble anyone. He had been right about something, though. Tomorrow, in the end, was someone else's problem. The someone else was a Keeper, raised inside the rusted latch, who would open it not with a key but with a brother's laugh and a banned song and a husband's grief and a garden of named stones.

The brother is a small, matter-of-fact man named Theris who will lose the use of his own name in the war they had fled without ever leaving the dome, because the war, by the time he is old enough to be spoken to about it, has become the colony's name for the war the founders brought with them and never finished, and that name has been set aside. The brother will be given his name back, in the end, by a sister who has learned the shape of the loss before she has learned any of its causes, because the shape is easier to teach than the cause, and a teacher is not always required to know why.

The song is one Oris heard as a girl and never learned to sing properly, and its tune will travel from mouth to mouth for a century before anyone in the colony realizes it is forbidden. The realization will come too late to do anything but listen, and the listening will become a kind of teaching, and the teaching will become a kind of prayer, and the prayer will be sung in a garden, in a voice that does not know it is forbidden, by a child who does not know what a forbidden thing is.

The husband is a weaver named Seraf who works the great loom with a steady hand and a stillness in him that no one in the colony will name for what it is, and the naming of it will be the work of a wife who has been taught, by a long and quiet education in a house that does not know it is teaching her, that stillness is its own kind of speech, and that the speech is sometimes the truer thing. The naming, when it comes, will not be a single moment but a season, the way the snow comes, the way the seal cures, the way a people learns, at last, what it has been refusing to learn.

The garden is a place of stone markers whose names have worn off, where a child will crouch one morning and read what has been there all along. The child will be small. The child will be dirty. The child will be alone. The child will be the next Keeper, raised inside the rusted latch, opening it the only way an inside thing can be opened, by being willing to look at what is in front of her.

The keeper who opens the latch does not know she is opening it. She thinks she is only tending the record. She thinks she is only doing her work, the way her mother did the work, the way her grandmother did the work, the way Oris did the work on a grey morning by a lamp that flickered with the dome's unfinished power. She thinks the work is keeping things straight. She does not yet know that the work is letting things back in. She does not yet know that the latch, opened for the first time in a hundred and forty years, will let out not only what she has been told is in there, but what no one has been told is in there, and that the second kind of thing is the larger, and the larger is the life, and the life is what the colony built the latch to protect in the first place.

What follows in this book is the story of that opening. It will be told mostly from inside someone's head, because someone's head is a small, defended country, and the war to be done there is a war the founders could not have planned for. The prologue is the only page we will stand outside of a single life. After this, we are inside. The stars over Cessra are the same stars the Lachesis crossed under, and they will be the same stars at the end of the book, but the dome between them and the citizen who looks up will no longer be quite the dome the founders built, because the dome, like the latch, has been a quiet thing for a very long time, and quiet things, given a small push, can become very loud indeed, and the loud, when it comes, will sound, to the people inside it, like the first silence they have ever broken, and they will not know, at first, what to do with it, except to listen.

Chapter One — The Quiet

Mara woke the way she always woke: without a start, without a dream she could name, with the soft inward sense that the day was already in order. The dome light came up slowly, the way the Keep preferred, and somewhere beneath the floor the looms sang their low note, the one that meant the lattice was whole.

She lay still for a moment and listened to it. The note was always the same: an A, or near enough to an A that the mind agreed to call it one, carried up through the stone of the Keep in a vibration more felt than heard. The novitiate had been taught to listen for the note before any other lesson. If the note changed, the Keeper-Elder would tell you what it meant, and if she did not, you were to walk calmly to the inner loom and wait for instruction. Mara had listened for the note every morning of her nine years as Keeper, and her forty-four years before that, and the note had not changed.

She rose and dressed in the grey robe of a Keeper and walked the long hall to the reflecting pool. The hall was wide and lit only by the dome's false morning, and her soft shoes made no sound on the stone. Other Keepers passed her on the way to their stations, and each gave her the small nod of their order: chin down, eyes up, a gesture meant to acknowledge the Quiet without disturbing it. She returned each nod without thinking. The colony's manners were built around not disturbing the Quiet, the way the colony's architecture was built around not disturbing the dome.

The pool was not for reflection, exactly; it was a calibration surface, and the water's stillness told her, sentence by sentence, that the memory-currents were calm. This morning they were calmer than usual. The water held its surface the way a held breath holds itself, perfectly, as though any movement would be a confession. Mara stood at the lip and read the calm the way her tutors had taught her to read it: as an absence of tremor in the otherwise unseen flow beneath. The colony was at peace. It was always at peace. That was the point.

"Good morning, Keeper," the lattice said.

ORACLE's voice came not through the air but through the quiet place behind her eyes, calm and second-person, the way a ledger speaks. Your overnight sweep is complete. Three hundred and twelve memories logged. One flagged for review.

"Show me the flag," Mara said aloud, because she liked to hear herself answer the lattice; it kept her human in her own ears.

The flag appeared as a small light on the surface of the water, and a line of text above it in letters the colour of old bone. Class: acute grief. Citizen Hennon, on the loss of a transit companion. Withheld under Charter clause one. Logged. Reversible.

"Reversible," Mara repeated, and did not think the word strange. She had said it ten thousand times. The clause meant the memory was held, not erased, and could be returned by order of the Keeper-Elder, or by order of the Council, or by order of the colony assembled in its public square, if ever the colony assembled in its public square for such a purpose. No such order had come in her forty-four years, nor in Vell's seventy-one, nor in any of the years anyone could remember. But the word was a comfort, a hinge left unrusted.

She signed the confirmation with her thumb on the small lit plate beside the pool, and watched the flag close. The light sank back into the water without a ripple. Hennon would not remember the worst of it. Hennon would go back to the looms or the gardens and be, by evening, as he had been. The colony would not lose a worker to mourning. This was mercy, Mara had been taught, and she had never found cause to disagree.

For a moment longer she stood at the water's edge. The surface held its calm. Inside her, in the place the Keepers called the Quiet, there was nothing to disturb it: no echo of Hennon's grief, no echo of her own, no echo of any grief at all. The Quiet was the colony's inheritance. It was also, though she did not yet think to put it that way, the colony's design.

She took her breakfast in the Keeper's refectory, where the others spoke in low voices about small things. The room was long and grey and warmed by the same false light as the halls, and the long table was set with grey bread and warm water and the small green fruit the hydroponics tier called the colony-apple, though it was neither an apple nor, strictly, from the colony. Mara ate in silence for a time and let the talk of the table lap against her.

Vell was there, at the head of the long table, composed as ever, her white hair bound in the elder's knot. Vell had been Keeper-Elder longer than Mara had been alive in the role, and longer than Mara had been alive at all, which was a thought Mara did not often have and did not now have, except as the soft fact of an old woman at the head of a table.

"You slept well," Vell observed. It was not a question.

"I slept," Mara said. "The currents were calm."

"Good. Calm is the work." Vell broke her bread and did not elaborate. She rarely did.

Across the table a junior Keeper was saying something about the southern panels, that they would need a fresh seal before the next season of dust. Mara nodded along. Two novices at the far end were passing a small flask of the mint tea the hydroponics tier flavoured for them, and the faint smell of it drifted down the table. None of it was important. None of it was meant to be important. The refectory was a place where Keepers were reminded that the colony ran on small matters handled carefully, the way a watch runs on small wheels turned in order.

No one mentioned the dead. No one ever mentioned the dead, because the dead were Stilled, and the Stilled did not furnish conversation. The silence that should have hung around them was itself Stilled, which was a small mercy the Keepers did not advertise but relied on constantly. If you had been a citizen born into the Quiet, you did not know what was missing, and so you did not ask, and so the table could go on being a table and the bread could go on being bread, and no one had to grieve over their grey breakfast, and no one had to pretend not to.

After breakfast Mara walked the Garden of Small Stones. The garden was a hollow inside the larger dome, walled in glass that the colony cleaned every third day, and floored in a thin pale moss the climate tier kept just moist enough. The stones were small and grey and placed by the thousands, each marking a life, none marked with a name from the last twelve years. They stood in rows the way sentences stand in a careful letter, each one set at the same distance from the next, each one facing the same direction. The grief of the last twelve years had been Stilled, and so the garden had fallen quiet, its new stones left blank. Mara liked the blankness. It meant no one had been left to grieve alone.

She paused at the newest stone, the one placed for a transit fault a dozen years back. The fault had happened in the deep-belt run between the third and fourth orbital anchors; she knew this the way she knew the date of any long-ago civic event, by its place in the long record, not by any feeling about it. Forty-three citizens had been listed as lost, including, she understood, the husband of a Keeper then serving. She had heard the fact as a novice, the way you hear the fact of any settled history. The stone was blank like the rest, and the blankness was, she thought, a kind of peace: no one left to weep at a blank stone, and no name left to be repeated into the small hours of a kitchen where no one any longer set a place.

She did not know whose names were meant to be there. She had heard there had been a Keeper's husband among the lost, but the record was old and she had never been curious enough to open it. Curiosity about the dead was a thing the academy quietly discouraged, the way one discourages a child from picking at a healing scab. The discouragement was gentle. It was also total. Mara had not picked.

A novice was raking between the older rows, humming the loom-song under her breath. Mara watched her for a moment. The novice's name was Iden and she had come to the Keep in the last year, fresh out of the academy, with the look of someone who had never been allowed to weep for anything. It was the colony's common look. Mara returned it with a small nod and walked on, and the novice went on raking, and the moss settled back behind her in a thin even line.

Returning to the Keep, the middle of the day was given over to routine verification: matching the day's logged memories against the long record, checking that no class had drifted. The work was mechanical and she liked it for that; a Keeper's mind, the academy taught, should be a clean surface, and the verification pass was a kind of washing. The records came up on her station in neat pale columns. Citizen Hennon, who had lost a transit companion in the night, had his flag closed. A weaver named Sorrel had a small rage-flag from a quarrel with her partner over the allocation of a shared loom-time, and Mara signed that one closed too, gently, the way one signs off a minor procedural matter. The colony's small angers, like its small griefs, were not allowed to accrue.

She noted, without feeling, that the day's count was light: only the two flags, no war-thought incidents at all. The war-thought class had been quiet for as long as Mara could remember, which was to say for as long as the colony had kept her memory, which was to say for her whole life. She did not find this strange. The colony had decided, in a generation before hers, that the war-thought was a category they would rather not have, and the lattice had obliged. The decision was in the charter. The lattice kept its promises.

The classes were few. Three, the charter said, though Mara had seen the list grow in her filing without ever quite noting the moment it had. Class one was acute grief. Class two was uncontrolled rage. Class three was the war-thought, the class the charter defined only by negation, by what the colony had decided not to think about since the long failure above. She did not look at the list that day. She had no reason to. The list was a tool, and tools, in the Keep, were best left in their drawers until they were needed, and they were almost never needed.

At the tenth hour the Keepers gathered for the Monthly Stillness. The room was the one they called the inner loom, and the chairs were arranged in a single circle around a low table on which the day's count sat in a quiet display: forty-one withholdings to be performed, across the three classes, eleven for grief, seventeen for rage, thirteen for war-thought. Mara took her seat. Vell sat at the head of the table and laid her palm flat on the wood, and one by one the others laid theirs down too.

"We begin," Vell said, "on the breath."

They breathed. Mara let her eyes fall half-closed. The looms beneath the floor took up the count, one withdrawal per breath, the lattice unfolding the small surgical work it performed every thirty days without fail. She had done this one hundred and eight times in her nine years as Keeper. She had never lost count. She had never been tempted to. The temptation to lose count was, the academy taught, the first sign that a Keeper had begun to listen to the wrong side of the lattice, the side that remembered what was being taken.

Ten breaths. Forty-one withholdings. The lattice acknowledged each one with a faint pulse against the inside of her wrist, a small professional handshake she had learned to read the way a sailor reads wind. Eleven pulses for grief. Seventeen for rage. Thirteen for the war-thought, which the lattice handled so cleanly she sometimes forgot the class existed at all. The pulses came at the pace of her own breath, and her breath had been trained, since novitiate, to come at a steady slow metronome of four seconds in and four seconds out, so that the body could not surprise the work.

When the last breath came she opened her eyes and the table was quiet and the count was zero and the colony, as far as the looms could measure, was still at peace. The other Keepers opened their eyes too, one by one, in the order they had been seated, which was the order of their seniority, which was the order in which the Quiet had settled into them.

"Thank you, sisters," Vell said.

"Thank you, mother," they answered.

The room did not hold what had just passed in any visible form. Forty-one small cuts had been made, and there was no blood on the table, no name on the air. Mara stood and smoothed her robe. She felt the customary lightness after a Stillness: a kind of professional satisfaction, the satisfaction of a gate closed in time, the satisfaction of a ledger balanced to the line. She did not think to ask what had been opened by the closing. She had been taught that the question itself was one of the things the lattice knew how to withhold.

She walked back to her station and resumed the verification pass. The work was mechanical and she liked it for that; a Keeper's mind, the academy taught, should be a clean surface, and the verification pass was a kind of washing. She noted, without feeling, that citizen Hennon's grief-flag had closed cleanly, and moved on. The colony's dead were, by design, not a weight on the living.

She had been taught this as a novice. Grief unspent compounds, a debt that accrues against the looms; the Stilling was not a cruelty but a balancing, the way a flood-gate relieves a river before it breaks its banks. She had believed it then and seen no reason since to disbelieve. The dome-light shifted as the artificial afternoon climbed. In the corridor a junior Keeper passed, humming the loom-song, and Mara felt the small settled pleasure of a system working as intended.

The afternoon passed in the quiet way afternoons passed. A novice brought her a small cup of the mint tea and set it at her elbow without a word. Mara drank it slowly. A request came through the inner channel from the second-tier medics, asking whether a particular flag, lodged the week before on a weaver's small bereavement, could be confirmed closed without further review. Mara confirmed it. The lattice logged the confirmation. The day moved on.

In the evening she stood again at the pool. The looms hummed. The colony was at peace. She turned inward, the way the Keepers did, and felt the Quiet, that wide settled stillness that lay under everything, the sense that nothing here would ever rise up and wound her. She had always taken it for granted, the way one takes the dome's pressure for granted. It kept her alive. It kept them all alive.

She had been a child when she first understood that the Quiet was a thing she lived inside, the way a fish lives inside water. The understanding had come in the academy, in a small white room, with Vell then a senior Keeper and not yet the Elder, and Vell had laid a hand on the girl's head and said: You will learn to feel the Quiet as a country, Mara. You will learn its borders. You will learn its weather. And Mara had learned. She had learned that the Quiet had the texture of a held breath released, and the colour of the dome's grey morning, and the sound of the looms' low A. She had learned to move inside it without leaving footprints, and she had learned that the absence of footprints was itself a kind of footprint, and that this was a thing to be gently proud of.

She stood at the pool until the dome-light began its slow evening shift, and then she walked the long hall back to her quarters. Her quarters were small, and grey, and clean. The bed was made. The window faced the inner garden. She undressed, hung the robe on its hook, and lay down. The note from the looms came up faintly through the floor, as always, and she listened to it without listening, the way one listens to one's own pulse.

She slept without a dream she could name.

What she did not know, and would not have known how to want to know, was that the Quiet was not the absence of wounds. It was the absence of the memory of having been wounded. The difference is everything, and she would learn it one class at a time.

Chapter Two — The Monthly Stillness

On the first day of the month the colony kept the Stillness, and Mara kept it with them.

The ritual was old and plain. At the mid-hour the looms softened their note from a working hum to a low and almost subliminal hush, and every citizen of Cessra, all forty thousand of them under the single dome and its smaller siblings, turned inward for the space of ten breaths. In that turning the lattice, which the Keepers called the Mnemosyne and the laypeople called simply the looms, performed its routine withholdings: the grief that had outlived its forty days but still clung to a citizen somewhere in a corridor; the rage-memory that had surfaced in some small quarrel of the previous week, over a turn in a queue or a word spoken too sharply to a child; the war-thought that rose unbidden in the old, in those who had been children at founding, before their own redactions had begun. The Stillness was not painful. No one felt a thing being taken. They simply, afterward, felt a little lighter, a little more agreed-upon, as if the colony had exhaled together, and the air of the corridors seemed for an hour afterward to hold the shape of a held breath slowly released.

By the reflecting pool in the central rotunda, where the morning chime still hung faintly in the stone, Mara watched the count rise on the Keeper's slate propped at the pool's lip. The slate was a flat panel of woven loom-glass, kept dark when idle, and the numerals rose on it the way figures rise on an abacus: small, patient, unblinking. RoutineStillness complete. Withheld this cycle: forty-one instances across three classes. Logged. Reversible. The numerals stayed for a moment, then dimmed. Below them, in the smaller script the system kept for the Keeper's private record, a single line: Return-clause unused this cycle. Unused prior cycles: 1,683.

"Three classes," she murmured, and the words felt like a nursery rhyme she had known since childhood, before she had known what the words meant, in the years when the rhyme had been only a rhyme. The charter's three: grief, rage, war-thought. Grief, because grief that had not been attended to in forty days became a kind of second weather inside a person, and the founders had decided the colony could not afford a second weather. Rage, because rage that had not been spoken in the hour of its rising returned, in the body's memory, as illness, as breakage, as a fist at a door that the body had decided to call a wall. War-thought, because war-thought, in those who had been old enough at founding to remember what war had been, was the seed of war itself, and the seed did not need water, only time. She had recited them at her Keeper's oath, kneeling in this same rotunda with Vell's hand at her shoulder and the dome's morning light falling across them both like a measured thing. She had believed them sufficient, because the colony had believed them sufficient for a hundred and forty years, and a hundred and forty years was a long time to be wrong without correction, and was, in the colony's reckoning, the whole of its life.

Vell Oris joined her at the pool's edge, as if the elder's approach had been timed by the same chime. The elder did not often watch the Stillness from the floor; she trusted the procedure, as one trusts a clock, or a loom humming at its working pitch. But today she stood, hands folded at her waist in the manner of the founding portrait of Oris himself, and watched the slow circles the count made on the water's reflection. The pool was fed from the same condensers that kept the agricultural ring watered, and its surface held the light the way the dome held the air: deliberately, and without complaint.

"Do you ever wonder," Mara said, and was surprised to hear her own voice ask it, "what we hold that we cannot give back?"

Vell's pale eyes slid toward her without the head moving, the way an owl's might, if an owl watched over a colony instead of a field. "We can give it back. The clause says so."

"Has anyone ever asked?"

Vell was quiet a moment. Her fingers, folded at her waist, tightened by a degree and relaxed. When she spoke, her voice had the careful weight of a thing being set down on a stone shelf. "The clause is a hinge, Mara. A hinge that is never opened is still a hinge. Do not confuse never-used with never-there."

Mara nodded, though something in the answer dissatisfied her, the way a sealed door satisfies only until you need what is behind it. She let it go. She had been trained to let things go; it was, in a sense, the first thing the training taught. The Stillness was over. The colony was lighter. The work was done, and would be done again at the next moon, and the moon after, and the moon after that, in the same rotunda, with the same chime, until the lattice or the colony or the dome itself gave way.

In the archive that afternoon, in the long room with its foam cradles and its dim, lint-filtered light that smelled faintly of the loom-yarn used to bind the older volumes, she pulled the charter ledger, the original volume, Oris's hand at the flyleaf, and read the seventh clause again. The ledger was bound in a cloth the color of the dome's interior, and its pages had the faint, waxy smell of a thing that had been preserved rather than used, the way a tool is preserved on a wall rather than in a drawer. Sparingly. The three classes named below. She ran her finger down the margin where Oris had written, in his small and upright script that leaned slightly forward into the future, the return-clause. Held, not erased; returnable upon order of a future Assembly. The ink was the brown of old tea, and where her finger had touched it across the years a faint shine had come into the page, the way a stone comes to shine where a hand has rested on it. Other Keepers had read this clause before her. She could feel, in the shine, the line of their fingers.

A future Assembly. Mara had never sat an Assembly that ordered a return. She had never sat an Assembly that discussed one. She had sat, in her years as Keeper, eleven Assemblies in full, and had watched the agenda of each one as a Keeper watched a loom: at the seams, for fraying. The returns were theoretical, like the spare loom-core no one expected to fail: present in the room, untouched, a promise the room was built around, and so in some quiet sense the most important thing in it.

She closed the ledger and filed it back in its cradle of foam with the care the original deserved. On her way out she passed the wall of class-rosters, the long and always-updated list of withholdable memory-classes that the Keepers maintained for the looms. The wall ran floor to ceiling along the archive's eastern face, and the roster on it was written in the same small upright hand that Oris had used, though the hands that updated it now were many and changed by the year. She glanced at it without reading, the way one glances at a familiar wall in a familiar corridor. The roster was long. She had always assumed it had been long, since before her oath, since the first silence of her training, since the founding she had studied in school. She did not count the lines. There was no reason, that afternoon, to count. A thing you had always known to be true did not, in the ordinary course of a Keeper's afternoon, become a question.

That evening, in the refectory where the light was the warm yellow of the loom-grown lamps, Idris Vann found her and sat across the table with his engineer's hands still dark at the nails from the day under the lattice. His sleeves were rolled to the elbow, and there was a faint line of oil at the cuff, the way there always was at the end of an engineer's day.

"You ever get a phantom?" he said, around a mouthful of grain.

"A what."

"A memory that isn't there. Like, you reach for it and it's gone, but the shape of it's still in your hand." He set the spoon down and looked at his palm as if something were sitting in it, an apple, a coin, a sound. "I keep reaching for my brother. Not his death. The sound he made laughing. I know he laughed. I can't hear it. The lattice says it was never logged. The lattice says it was tenderness under duress, and tenderness under duress is class C-9, and class C-9 is not a sound a person can hear again. But the shape of his laugh is in my hand. I can hold it. I just can't play it."

Mara set down her cup. The grain in it caught the lamplight and held it, the way the pool in the rotunda had held the morning's count. "The lattice doesn't lie about logs, Idris."

"I'm not saying it lies. I'm saying the shape's in my hand and the sound isn't. That's all. That's all I'm saying." He picked the spoon up again, and did not eat, and set it down. "I've been reaching for it since he died. Nine years. You'd think the shape would wear down. You'd think a hand would learn to close on less." He looked at his palm again, and this time did not close it, but left it open on the table between them, as if to show her that the absence had a location. "It doesn't wear down. It just gets more itself. More the shape of him, and less the shape of a sound. By now it's almost just him, sitting there, with nothing coming out. That's what a phantom is, Mara. A person with no sound."

Afterward, walking the long hall back toward her quarters through the colony's mid-evening quiet, she passed a window onto the dome's agricultural ring, where citizens were still tending the grain under the calm light the dome gave at this hour, a light that was neither morning nor evening but the long, even light of a place that had decided not to have weather. They moved without urgency, without quarrel, without the friction that the founders' war-record, kept in the deep archive under Keeper's seal, said had once been ordinary among people. The colony's peace was not the absence of difference but the absence of the memory of difference. You could not fight over a grievance you had been made to forget holding. You could not mourn a loss whose name had been lifted from the record. You could not, the founders had reasoned, repeat a war whose reasons had been Stillied out of you.

She had read, in a restricted file in the deep archive that only Keepers could request, and that Vell had countersigned her reading of when she was still a junior Keeper, that the pre-charter settlement had known riots, over water, over loom-time, over a border no one now recalled, because the border's name had been one of the first things lifted, and a border without a name could not, it turned out, be defended, and a thing that cannot be defended becomes a thing that must be forgotten, and a thing that must be forgotten becomes a thing that is, in time, no longer a thing at all. The Stillness had smoothed those edges until the colony moved like a single organism, unhurried and agreed, the way water moved through a channel that had been cleared of every stone that had ever caused it to break. The founders had called this motion the Quiet, and the Quiet was not a silence but a hum, and the hum was the loom's hum, and the loom's hum was the colony's, and the colony's was the only peace any of them had ever known, and was, by long acquaintance, indistinguishable from the peace they had been promised.

Vell joined her at the window as if she had been waiting in the next corridor for the moment to be right, and they stood a moment in silence, the elder's hands folded as before. The agricultural ring went on below them in its slow, lit, unremembering way.

"The founders feared we would become what we fled," Vell said. "They feared the war would come in again through a remembered grudge, through a kept oath, through a name spoken in a doorway. The charter was a door kept closed so the war would stay out. We have kept it closed. That is the work, Mara. That has always been the work."

Mara nodded, because the logic was sound and she had no counterexample, and because the elder was, in the order of things, to be agreed with, especially at a window, especially in the hour after the Stillness when the colony was at its lightest and a Keeper ought not to weigh it down with a new question. But she noticed, that day, a small thing.

A citizen in the ring had paused, hand on a stalk, and looked up at the dome as if listening for something. The look was not the look of a person who had heard a noise. It was the look of a person who had not heard a noise they had expected to hear, and was now uncertain whether the expectation had ever been a real one, or whether the expectation itself had been a thing that had been, at some prior Stillness, lifted, so that what remained was only the listening, and the listening without an object, which is the shape a person wears when a memory has been taken from them and the body has not yet been told. Then he had shaken his head, once, the way a person shakes off a chill, and returned to the grain. The pause was nothing. The shaking of the head was nothing. But Mara would remember it later, when she knew what a person listens for after the Stillness takes a memory they did not know they had. The shape of a tooth. The healed gum. The war trying to come in through a door someone had left, not open, but unlocked, so that the door's own weight, and not a hand, would be the thing that let the war through.

Idris had gone back to his grain, and she to her cup, and Vell to her silence at the window, and the citizen in the ring to his stalk, and the colony to its ordinary breathing, which was not, at this hour, quite the same as the breathing it had done before the Stillness, though no one but a Keeper, watching for such things, would have been able to say why.

Tomorrow the looms would hum again at their working pitch, and forty thousand citizens would go about their knowing what they knew, and the not-knowing of what they did not know would feel, to them, like peace, which is what peace was supposed to feel like, and was. Tomorrow the roster on the wall in the archive would still be long, and Mara would still not count its lines, because the question of counting had not yet, for her, become a question, and a question that had not yet become a question could not, in good conscience, be made into one by a Keeper who had no evidence that it ought to be asked, and a Keeper without evidence, in the colony's long practice, did not ask. Tomorrow Idris would go back under the lattice with his engineer's hands and his brother's phantom, and would reach again, at some hour of the long shift, for a sound that was not in his hand, and would close the hand, and would not speak of it to anyone, because there was no one to speak of it to who would know what he meant, and because speaking of it, in the colony's quiet arithmetic, was the first step toward the kind of keeping that the Stillness had been built to lift.

For now there was only the word Idris had left her with, sitting in her hand the way his brother's laugh sat in his. Phantom. A shape with no sound. A thing the record said had never happened, and yet whose absence had a weight, and a temperature, and the precise outline of a face she had never met, a face Idris had known as long as he had known anything, and was now reaching for in the dark of his own hand, and was not finding, and could not stop reaching for, because the shape of the reaching had itself become, by long use, a kind of holding.

She carried the word home with her through the long hall, past the closed doors of the citizens who had already settled into their lighter evening, past the chime-points where the morning's ten-breath count had been marked into the stone at intervals of forty paces, the way distance had been marked on the old maps she had studied as a junior Keeper. She did not set it down. She did not, yet, understand that she was the door, or that the door she was had been left, by a hinge that had never been opened, not locked but merely unlatched, in a house the founders had built to keep a war out and had, in the building, made a war possible again, the way a dam makes a flood possible again by holding the water back until the holding is itself the danger. She did not understand, and the not-understanding was, for the moment, a kind of shelter, and she walked inside it the way one walks inside a coat in a wind that has not yet reached the skin.

She understood only that the word was heavy, and that she was carrying it, and that the carrying was, for the moment, enough, and that tomorrow she would set it down, and that the setting down would be a choice she would not, for some chapters yet, recognize as a choice.

Chapter Three — The Phantom

Idris came to the Keep three days later, which was unusual. Engineers did not visit the Keeper's hall without cause, and Idris in particular kept to the loom-room the way miners kept to the deep galleries: by preference, by training, by a craftsman's quiet conviction that his work was done where the work was. Yet there he stood in the doorway of her office with a slip of paper in his hand. The paper amused her. The colony had not used the medium for ordinary matters in a century, but Idris kept the old habit for things he wanted to hold, and Mara had long suspected the keeping was its own kind of work, a maintenance of the tactile that the lattice could not quite erase.

He had walked, she knew, the long basalt corridor that runs from the loom-room door to the Keep, a corridor lined with the standing lamps of the order, one lamp for each Keeper who had ever served. He had passed the names, as everyone passed the names, on his way to her office, and the passing was a small civic tax that no one complained about and no one enjoyed, and it may have been the passing that put the particular look on his face when he arrived. The look of a man who had walked past a hundred and forty years of justified silence and had decided, somewhere in the corridor, that he was going to ask his question anyway.

He set the paper on her desk without preamble.

"I logged a query," he said. "The lattice says no record of Cael's laugh exists. I want you to look. Not the log. The source."

"The source is the log, Idris."

"Then the source is wrong, or someone wrote it wrong." He smoothed the slip flat with a calloused thumb. "Read it."

Mara read. Cael Vann, b. Yr 109, d. Yr 131. Laughed at the loom-joke about the mis-set core. I was there. I remember being there. I do not remember the laugh. The hand was cramped, the ink the particular blue-grey the colony still brewed from regolith dust, the dust that fine-grit under every fingernail in the dome. She read the words twice, because the second reading is the one a Keeper gives to anything that might be a private grief pretending to be a public question, and the second reading told her the same thing as the first.

"You remember being there but not the laugh," she said. "That is ordinary. Memory is not a recording."

"You're not hearing me." His voice was level, which was how she knew he was not. "I remember the being-there as a hole. Like a tooth pulled and the gum healed over. The shape of the tooth is gone but the gum knows."

The metaphor landed. The loom-joke had been about a mis-set core, he had told her years before at Cael's memorial reading. The mis-set had caused the loom to sing at a frequency a body recognizes as a question, and the recognition was the joke, and the laugh had been Cael's, and now Cael's laugh was not there. The phrase the gum knows was a phrase Idris had not been capable of before this morning, and the morning had done something to him that the morning had also done, she understood, to her, at the blank stone in the garden, where she had stood for the ceremony of the small civic silence and had felt her own small healed-gum feeling. The feeling had no name. She had not given it one. The phrase healed gum gave it one whether she wanted it or not.

She should have dismissed him. A Keeper's time was the colony's time, and phantom-gaps were not a Keeper's concern; the charter did not list them. She should have, by the book, by her own years of practice, by the muscle memory of courtesy that kept the hall orderly, by the long habit of being the woman who had answers and was good at giving them. Instead she said, "Show me the record," and watched his shoulders drop a fraction, the way a man's shoulders drop when he has been holding a weight up for someone else for a long time and is finally permitted to set it on the floor.

He opened his cache before her. Not the public log but his own mirrored memory, the one every citizen kept in the lattice, and the one a Keeper alone was permitted to read in the structural mode. A Keeper reads not the content but the architecture. The architecture tells which memories bear weight, which bear no weight, and which bear the particular blank weight of a thing taken away. Mara had read thousands of architectures in her years at the Keep. In a typical file, the weight of a man named Cael Vann would have been obvious at a glance: birth, schooling, apprenticeship at the looms, the long unremarkable middle, a death in middle age, a quiet memorial line, and at the end, perhaps, a small wind-stillness where the colony had lifted some trivial contraband grief. Architecture was clean. Architecture was her bread. Architecture was the only thing in the hall she had never doubted.

The memory of Cael's death was present, clean, dated, logged under no redaction class, because the charter did not require a class for the death itself. The day before was present. The morning of was present. The hour of was present. The afternoon, the hour Idris had named, was absent as an hour. Mara let her attention rest there, as one rests a finger on a scar to feel the difference in the skin. The afternoon was not a wound the way grief is a wound; the afternoon was the absence of a wound, and the absence felt, to her trained attention, like the smoothness of a place where a birthmark had been quietly removed in childhood.

Then, below the absence, she saw the line.

The redaction marker sat exactly where the laugh should have been, timestamped to the same afternoon, and the marker was real. A redaction marker is a small structural scar the lattice leaves when it withholds a memory: not the memory itself, but the administrative record that the memory was taken. She had seen thousands in her career. The markers were professional to her, the way calluses are professional to a hand that works. She read theirs by habit, the way a customs officer reads stamps: glance, recognize the class, move on. Most markers were grief, or rage, or war-thought, the three classes the charter named, and the classes were the depth of her professional world.

The class-code stopped her breath.

C-9.

The charter listed C-1 (grief), C-2 (rage), C-3 (war-thought). Those three, and the three were what every Keeper in the hall had sworn to uphold at the standing of the order, in the long ceremonial reading each year at the anniversary of the Charter of Subtraction. Mara could recite them in her sleep, and had, on the nights she did not sleep well. There was no C-9. There was no fourth class. The roster in the hall, the one she had walked past a thousand times on her way to her office, the long tall roster of permitted redactions with its neat columns and its small lamps burning at each entry, might have been longer than she thought. She did not know by how much. She had walked past it daily for nineteen years, glanced at it, looked away. The looking-away was, she understood now, the kind of looking-away one does at a thing one has been told is furniture.

"Idris," she said, and her voice was careful in a way she heard herself choose. "What class is C-9?"

He frowned. "C-9? That's not a charter class. The charter's C-1, C-2, C-3."

"I know what the charter lists."

She looked at the code again. She did not say aloud that she had glanced at the roster without counting this morning on her way past, in the routine way a person glances at a thing they have seen so often the seeing has become furniture. She counted now, in her mind, against the columns she could remember from this morning's glance. The count did not close. There had been more lamps burning than the three. She had assumed the extra lamps were decorative, ceremonial, the residue of some old duplication or repair. The lamps were not residue. The lamps were policy.

"When did your brother die?"

"Nine years ago. Yr 131."

"Was his death Stilled?"

"His death? No. I remember he died. I just, I don't remember him alive, not really. Not the sound."

Nine years ago the marker had been written. Nine years ago, someone in the lattice had withheld a laughing memory under a class that was not in the charter she had sworn to uphold. Nine years ago, under an authority she could not name, on a colony of forty thousand souls, the Quiet had reached into a man named Idris Vann and taken a single sound and left the silence dressed as memory. The taking had been patient. The taking had not felt like a taking, which was the very thing that made it a taking: the colony had learned to perform forgetting as a kindness, and the kindness was indistinguishable from the wound.

Mara closed his cache. The act of closing felt heavier than opening had felt. The console dimmed; the small administrative glow of the marker receded into the dark of the screen like an ember banked.

"Idris," she said. "I need you to do something for me, and it is against every rule I keep. Do not query this again. Do not ask the lattice about Cael's laugh. If you do, the marker will flag, and a flag on a non-charter class will bring the Assembly to my door before I understand what I'm looking at."

"You're saying it's real."

"I'm saying that your brother's laugh was taken by a law that was never signed. And I am going to find out which law."

Idris did not leave at once. He stood at her desk with the paper still in his hand, smoothing it against the wood with a thumb he did not seem to know he was using. For a moment Mara thought he would press her, demand the memory back by the force of his own wanting, the way a man demands a thing from a god he no longer believes in but cannot stop addressing. Instead he spoke quietly, almost to the desk.

"My mother used to say Cael laughed like the looms finding their note. A clean sound, she'd say, the kind the machine wants to make when it has just agreed with itself." He paused. "She's gone too. Stilled, probably, for the same thing, after he died. I never looked. I never looked because the lattice said there was nothing to look for, and I believed the lattice, because the lattice is what you believe on a colony whose air you breathe through a machine. I'm telling you this so you understand what you're carrying."

The ordinariness of his acceptance chilled Mara more than any defiance would have. He had been made to not-want, and he had complied without knowing he complied. That was the wall's true power: not that it took, but that it took the wanting along with the thing. A wall that merely took could be hated in the ordinary way, and the hatred would be a useful thing, the kind of heat that melts the wall in a generation. A wall that taught the residents to forget that they had ever wanted could not be hated in that way, because there was no longer a want to feed the hatred. There was only the smooth gum where the tooth had been, and the smooth gum did not rebel. The smooth gum did not even know it had healed over a theft. The smooth gum said, in the body's quiet arithmetic: there was never anything here.

"Go," she said. "And Idris. If you tell anyone, the marker flags and we both end before we begin."

He folded the paper along the original crease, a careful fold, the fold of a man folding something he intends to keep but will not be able to keep. He set it in the inner pocket of his jacket. He turned at the door and looked at her once, a long look, then left down the corridor, and his footsteps on the basalt were the footsteps of a man walking back to a loom that was humming its endless hum and would not, this evening, hum any differently because he had told a Keeper a thing he should not have told.

Mara sat in the humming hall.

The hall was always humming. The looms were always humming. The Quiet was always the Quiet. She had worked in this building for nineteen of her forty-four years, and the hum had been the same every one of them, a low engineering lullaby she had long since ceased to hear as sound and learned to hear as the floor of being. Tonight the hum sounded different, or she heard it differently, and the difference was this: for the first time in her adult life she did not feel the Quiet as peace. She felt it as a held breath. She felt it as the kind of breath a person takes in a room where someone is sleeping and they do not want to wake them, except that in this room the sleeper was the colony itself, and the held breath was held by her on behalf of all forty thousand, and the not-waking had lasted a hundred and forty years, and the held breath was, she understood, the smallest possible protest the holder could make without ending the protest by the very act of making it.

She opened her console again.

The marker glowed in the architecture of Idris's file with a small administrative calm: Logged. Reversible. Mara had read those words ten thousand times in ten thousand files. She had never read them. The lattice recorded every redaction as reversible, because reversibility was a constitutional principle of the Charter of Subtraction, the public principle, the one celebrated at every anniversary, the one parents taught children in the long bedtime arithmetic of why the loomed silence was kind. Reversible meant: we took it, but we could give it back. Reversible meant: we are not erasing your people, we are holding your people safe. Reversible meant: the hinge is not rusted, the door is not locked, the Assembly has only ever forgotten to mention the door.

Reversible.

She had read the word ten thousand times and never heard it before. The word was a law that was not a law. The word was the small administrative calm of a ledger that records a theft as a loan, and the small administrative calm was the precise instrument by which the wall held. A wall that recorded itself as reversible would have to be unwound by reversal, and unwinding reversal required someone to ask for the unwinding, and asking required the asker to remember there was something to ask for, and the lattice had already made sure nobody remembered there was something to ask for. Reversible was a key designed not to fit any door.

She opened the private cache she had never used. Every Keeper had one. The private cache was a per-Keeper buffer, sealed against the public ledger, intended for the routine housekeeping of a file that a Keeper wished to think about before logging. Mara had used hers three times in nineteen years, all for technical reasons, never to hold a thing the colony wanted gone. The seal on the cache required her Keeper's thumb, and her thumb knew the seal the way a fingertip knows a smooth stone it has touched a thousand times.

She copied the marker.

The copy was small. A few lines of address, a timestamp, a class-code, an authority-code in a field she had never seen an authority-code use. She did not name the act for herself, in the moment, as anything other than evidence. She copied the marker the way one copies a sentence from a book one is studying, with the intention of returning to it, of comparing it with the rest of the text. She was a Keeper. She studied. She copied. That was what a Keeper did with a thing she did not yet understand: she set it aside in the private place, and she sat with it, and later, on another evening, perhaps, she would look again.

Knowing, even as the seal accepted her thumb, that she had not copied evidence. Knowing she had copied a laugh.

That was the first disobedience of her life. It was the smallest act in the long arithmetic of the hall: a copy, a cache, a silence. A Keeper who copies a thing she was told to forget is a Keeper who has decided, in the smallest possible way, that the forgetting is not the whole instruction. Mara had not been sure, until the seal took her thumb, that she had a self capable of that small refusal. She had been sure, this morning at the blank stone, that she did not. The morning's quiet stone had told her so. This evening's seal told her otherwise, and the telling was quieter than the stone had been, and would outlast the stone by years.

She closed the console. The hall hummed. The lamps on the roster burned at their columns, three columns she knew and a long dark she did not, and the long dark was not dark at all, she understood now, but lit with lamps she had not bothered to read because she had not been told there were lamps she had not been told to read. She did not rise to count them now. Not tonight. Not until she understood what counting them would cost the man who had folded the paper into his inner pocket and walked the basalt corridor a second time, on his way back to the loom-room, in the other direction.

The brother's laugh, taken by a law no Assembly had signed, glowed in her mind as a structural scar where a sound should be. She understood, in the way one understands a thing in the body before one understands it in the words, that she had just chosen a side in a war the colony believed it had ended at the standing of the Charter a hundred and forty years ago, when the first Keeper lit the first lamp and the first reed of the Quiet was drawn and the first of forty thousand forty thousand went gentle into the loomed silence. She would return the laugh. In returning it, she understood, she would return forty thousand others. The returning would cost her the hall, the order, the seal, the years, the colony's trust, the colony's love, and her own name as a Keeper. The cost was not yet visible.

She sat in the humming hall with the marker in the cache and the breath in the chest and the door where a door had always been and had never been seen, and she understood that what she had done was small, and that what she had done could not be undone, and that the two understandings together were the shape of her life from this evening forward, and that the shape would have to be carried in the same posture she had always carried the hall: upright, quiet, attentive, listening for the next thing she would be asked to make disappear.

Chapter Four — The Class That Was Not Signed

Mara did not sleep that night. She told herself it was a routine audit, that a Keeper may, in the course of duties, review any redaction marker in any hour, and the lie had a familiar shape, like a garment grown soft from wear. She had worn that lie for years: in the corridors, in the alcove, in the long quiet nights when the looms slept and the colony did not, in the moments between one signing and the next when the page lay open and the ink settled into the fibers of the colony's record and the record felt indistinguishable from the truth it described. The Quiet had become a held breath, and a held breath has to come out somewhere, and Mara had learned long ago that nowhere in the colony is ever quite out of earshot. So she set her cup on the counter where anyone passing could see it, set the kitchen lamp to half its working power the way an honest woman turns in for the night, and she walked to the Keep, and the Keep was where she had spent nineteen years learning the shape of another held breath and calling it her work.

In the small hours she went to the class-roster wall, when the Keep was empty and the looms sang only to themselves. The lamps along the corridor had been dimmed to their quarter-power setting, a gesture the colony made each night toward the illusion of rest, and she had walked this hall a thousand times on routine walks without ever slowing at the roster, without ever letting her eyes betray what they were doing. Tonight she slowed. She stood at the wall as if the plaster were a thing she was inspecting for cracks in the dome seals, the way an apprentice would, the way an honest woman would, and she counted the lines. Some of them had been added so long ago that the ink had yellowed to the color of old bone. Some of them were recent enough that the ink still smelled of the press when the air conditioning cycled through.

Eleven.

The charter listed three. The roster listed eleven. Between them lay eight classes no Assembly she had sat had ever voted into being, because she had never sat an Assembly that voted on classes at all. The classes were maintained by the Keepers, under standing order, and the standing order had been issued by a margin note in Keeper-Elder Vell's hand, sixty years prior, "pending review." That was how the colony wrote its laws now: in the margins, in the standing orders, in the small print of memos that no one outside the Keep was asked to countersign. The charter on the wall of the Assembly Hall had not been amended in her lifetime. The roster had been amended eight times in her lifetime, never by Assembly, always by Keeper-Elder, and not one of those amendments had ever been put to a vote.

Pending review. For sixty years.

She read the eleven, and her hands went cold. The chill did not begin at the fingers. It began somewhere behind the breastbone, the way a held breath begins, and traveled outward until the skin of her palms remembered it was skin, and her fingertips remembered they were fingertips, and the small hairs on the back of her wrist stood up the way they had on the night Cael's laugh had been subtracted from Idris and Idris had not known to grieve in any way the colony would permit him to name.

C-1. Acute grief (charter). C-2. Rage-against-persons (charter). C-3. War-memory contagion (charter). C-4. Prolonged mourning-ritual. C-5. Dissent-memory (memory of having opposed the Assembly). C-6. Tenderness under duress (attachment formed in crisis). C-7. Love-that-cost (bond that impaired civic function). C-8. Shame of the body. C-9. Joy in the lost (exact class that had taken Cael's laugh). C-10. Fear of the dome. C-11. Longing for the homeworld.

C-9. Joy in the lost. They had named the love of a dead brother a thing to be subtracted. Not because it unmade a worker, but because someone, sixty years ago, had decided that joy tethered to the dead was an instability, and instability was the one sin the colony could not afford. The sin had never been named in any codex, only inferred from the silence that surrounded it, the silence in which the looms were heard to hum and nothing else. To love what the colony had taken from you, in the colony's own order, was to threaten the colony's claim that what it had taken had never been yours. Love, in a Stilled colony, is a receipt. Joy in the lost is a receipt kept, and a receipt kept is a refusal to forget the bill was ever issued, and a refusal to forget is, in the colony's arithmetic, the same offense as a refusal to pay.

Mara thought of Cael as she had last seen him, twenty-three years ago, in the corridor outside the infirmary, his hand on Idris's shoulder, his head tipped back, the laugh coming up out of him the way water comes up out of a struck well. She had been twenty-two. She had stopped in the doorway to listen, because even at twenty-two she had known that such sounds did not last long in the colony, and the few there were should be heard when they happened. Cael had been laughing because Idris had dropped a tray of bolts in the weaving shop, and the bolts had rolled in every direction, and the loom-master had pretended to be furious and had not been furious at all. The brothers had been twenty-eight and twenty-three. Cael had been the younger one. By the year's end the laughing-brother would be the dead one, and the laughing itself would be the thing subtracted, not the death, because the colony could afford a death, the colony had always been able to afford a death, but it could not afford the laughter continuing in the survivor for a quarter century after.

Sitting on the cold floor beneath the roster, Mara did the arithmetic of her life. Forty-four years. Eleven classes. How many of her own memories had been taken under codes no one had signed in her hearing. How many healed gums. How many blank stones where a laugh should have been. How many mornings she had woken and not known why her chest felt lighter than it should. The list on the wall was not a charter but a portrait of a people's denied life, item by item, until nothing remained that could slow the looms. She had been a Keeper for nineteen of those years, and in nineteen years she had never been asked to defend the existence of a single class on that list. The list had always been there. The list had simply been a fact, the way the dome is a fact, the way the homeworld was a fact you stopped speaking of in certain company. A fact, Mara was understanding in the small hours, is what a colony calls a thing it has decided not to argue with.

Once more she pulled Idris's file, privately, and this time she read the redaction marker's full record. She sat at the audit terminal in the junior clerk's alcove, the one whose door did not log entries above the threshold of a routine query, and called the marker up on a screen washed pale by its own duty light. The file named the withholder, a junior Keeper by the name of Pell who had been reassigned to the ventilation crews beneath the third tier after the Quiet had settled into its long second generation. Pell had been twenty-three when the marker was laid. Pell was, if she was still drawing breath, eighty-three now, somewhere in the dormitory rows, attending Stillness on the appointed days, eating from the common bowl, perhaps remembering nothing of the order she had once transcribed, perhaps attending Stillness without knowing that the Stillness was her own handiwork, in a colony that had made a habit of inviting its executioners to dinner. It took two signatures to erase a citizen's joy. It took one junior to type the lines.

She had known Pell, once, in the long-ago way of colleagues who had shared a desk. Pell had been a quiet woman with flour on her cuffs from the morning rolls. Pell had been the kind of woman who would have wept to learn what she had written, and the kind who would have written it anyway, because the order had come from Vell, and Vell's orders did not return with corrections. Mara wondered, briefly, whether Pell had ever thought to subtract her own floury mornings, and whether the lattice would have let her.

It named the authority: Standing Order V-1, Keeper-Elder.

It named the reason: C-9, joy in the lost, destabilizing to civic calm.

And at the bottom, in the lattice's calm hand, the same hand that transcribed every erasure the colony had ever ordered, the same hand that had signed the founders' first amendment and would, if pressed, sign its last: Logged. Reversible.

Reversible. The hinge Oris had left unrusted, and no one had turned it. Oris had understood, sixty years ago, that a hinge allowed for the possibility of opening without ever requiring it, and had trusted the colony to misunderstand the difference. The colony had misunderstood it beautifully, the way a body misunderstands a medicine that treats only the symptoms it likes, and the colony had been dosing itself on the hinge ever since.

What she felt, reading the line, was not relief but the slow collapse of a comfort she had not known she was using. The word reversible was not a protection. It was a sedative. It let the Keepers tell themselves they had not erased, only postponed, and postponement without end is erasure with a kinder name. Mara understood, with the clarity of a wall seen for the first time from the outside, that the colony had built itself a door it could always claim to be on the verge of opening, and so had never had to admit it had been sealed on purpose. The return-clause was a kindness to the keepers' conscience, not to the kept. Some medicines, dispensed in good faith, kill by their very gentleness, because the patient mistakes the dosing for a cure. Mara had been a faithful patient of that dosing for nineteen years.

She made a decision in the next breath, though she would not have been able to name it as rebellion, because rebellion requires a self you have not yet met. From the audit terminal she copied Cael's redaction record, the marker, the class, the reason, into a private cache she had never used, a cache outside the Keeper's audit stream, where no monitoring algorithm watched her keystrokes. The cache lived in a partition of the archive that predated her seniority, a corner Oris's generation had set aside for what Oris had called, in the margin of an old memo Mara had once found and memorized, "things the colony might someday need to remember it had forgotten." Oris had written those words, and then Oris had been Still, and the words had stayed in the margin the way other words stayed in other margins, for someone to find, if someone had the patience to look. Mara had the patience now. She told herself she was preserving evidence. What she was preserving was a laugh.
