No record of the eight classes between the charter's three and the roster's eleven gave a year of origin, but the margin where a date should have been held a kind of archaeology of its own, the way a tide line tells you something of the sea even when the sea is gone. C-4 had been added after a plague-year funeral that had slowed the looms for a fortnight of wailing in the dormitory corridors, the wailing of people who had come in their nightclothes to sleep on the floor beside the bed of a friend who was no longer being subtracted from, only being grieved. C-6 had come in after a vote that split the dome along fault lines nobody admitted were also kinship lines. C-7 had followed a love-triangle that ended in a sealed record and three looms unmanned for an entire season, the workers unable to pass one another in the corridors without a grief that would not be classed. C-9 had come in the year Idris lost his brother, and Idris's brother had laughed. The classes were not arbitrary. They were the colony's wounds, named and muted one by one so that the wound would not slow the work, and the work was the only thing the colony had agreed to value since the second generation, when the founders died and their grief was filed under C-1 and the looms went on humming through the vigil.

Now Mara understood why Vell had warned her, years before, that no Assembly had the stomach to ratify what it practiced. The colony had outgrown its charter and chosen to live the contradiction rather than speak it, because speaking it would require admitting the charter had failed, and admitting that is a destabilization the colony had been Stilled to avoid. A loop, then. The Stilling prevented the destabilization that would reveal the Stilling was unlawful, which prevented the destabilization that would reveal the Stilling was unlawful. The wall held itself up by the fear of its own falling, and she had just put her hand to the plaster and felt it give. The feeling was not unlike what she imagined a Stillness felt like from inside the ring: a pressure equal on all sides, and then a hairline crack, and then the room breathing.

In the morning she reported to Vell that the routine audit was complete and had found no anomalies. The elder looked at her for a long moment, her pale eyes reading something Mara hoped she could not see, the way an old keeper reads a loom that has begun to slip a thread. Vell had been Keeper-Elder for thirty-one of Mara's forty-four years, and in all that time she had not once raised her voice in the Keep, and in all that time Mara had trusted her the way one trusts a wall one has never tested. Vell's hair was the color of unbleached linen. Vell's hands rested on the desk as if they had been placed there for inspection. Vell said only, "Good. The currents were calm."

They were not calm. Mara could feel the breath she was holding, and she understood, with a clarity that was its own kind of wound, that she would not be able to let it out by forgetting. She had been a woman trained to set down burdens by laying them in the lattice's hands, and the lattice's hands were Vell's hands, and Vell's hands had signed the order. Laying a burden in those hands had been, until this morning, indistinguishable from laying it down.

Returning to her alcove after the report, she read the list of eleven a second time, and then a third, because the mind rejects what the conscience cannot yet carry. C-10, fear of the dome. C-11, longing for the homeworld. They had classified a citizen's terror of the fragile ceiling over their heads as a thing to be subtracted, and a citizen's love of the world they had fled as a thing to be subtracted, and neither class appeared in any charter any Assembly had signed in her hearing. The margin named Vell, and Mara felt the peculiar vertigo of discovering that the wall she had tended all her life had been built by a hand she respected, for a reason she could almost sympathize with, and was nonetheless a wall. She thought of the founders' riot-record, and of Vell's warning, and she saw the mechanism plainly now and hated herself a little for seeing it so late, because lateness is a kind of complicity the colony had never had to ask her to give. Every audit she had signed in nineteen years had been a vote she had not known she was casting.

Late that night she returned to the cache and added a line of her own, dated, beneath Cael's copied record: the first line in the hidden file that no Assembly had ordered and no Keeper's audit would ever log. She wrote it in a hand that was not the lattice's hand. She wrote it in the cadence of a woman who had begun, somewhere between the floor beneath the roster and the morning report, to keep a record of what it felt like to keep no record at all. She wrote the date the colony used, the year of the colony, the lunar reckoning of the second century; and she signed it with her own name, and for the first time in her Keeper's life her name meant something other than the colony had meant it to mean. The line was short. It named no class. It said only that a Senior Keeper of the Cessra archive, on this day, in this hour, had copied a record that the colony had ordered hidden, and had done so knowing it was hidden, and had done so anyway, and that the copy was kept now in a place the colony did not know existed. It was, in its modesty, the first act of dissent in the colony's second century that did not pretend to be something else. She did not know, as she wrote, that by adding one dated line to a hidden place she had become something the colony had no name for, a Keeper of what had been taken. The colony called such people nothing, because the colony had arranged to ensure there were none.

For a long time after she wrote it, Mara sat with her hands flat on the desk and her eyes on the line and did not move. Outside the alcove the looms sang to themselves. She listened to them the way one listens to a wall one has lived beside for nineteen years, with the sudden attention of a person hearing the wall for the first time as a wall and not as a piece of the room. The wall was singing. The room was the colony. The colony had been her room. The colony was no longer her room. She had changed rooms without changing corridors. That, she understood now, was the first move of any quiet emigration: to notice, without yet moving, that the doors had always been unlocked.

Counting is the first disobedience of a Keeper trained not to count. The self that met her in the counting was a Keeper no longer of what the colony ordered kept but of what it ordered forgotten, and the inversion was complete the moment she wrote the line, and the line would become the indictment, and the indictment would become the seed, and the seed would become the Understory, and the Understory would become the key, and the key would become the door, and the door would become the wind, and the wind would become the air, and the air would be breathed by a free colony, and the colony would be free. Mara sat very still in the alcove, and outside the alcove the looms sang to themselves, and she let the breath out at last, and it was not the colony's breath, and it was hers.

Chapter Five — The Elder's Warning

Vell sent for her on the fourth day.

The Keeper-Elder's quarters were ordered to a degree that bordered on the devotional. Nothing out of place. The grey robe folded exactly across the back of the reading chair, where Vell had placed it that morning at the same minute she placed it every morning, because the minute was itself a kind of class. The single window aligned so the dome-light fell across the desk at a fixed angle, where Vell had moved it decades ago and where it had remained, because to move it would have meant admitting a preference, and a preference was a kind of remembering, and remembering was what Vell had spent her life teaching the colony to do without. A small clay cup sat at the corner of the desk, holding a pen, holding nothing else; the cup had been Vell's mother's, brought up on the second ship, and the bringing up was the only inheritance Vell had ever spoken of to anyone, and the speaking of it had been in another room, decades ago, to a Mara who had not yet understood what was being given her.

Vell did not rise when Mara entered. She indicated the chair with a glance, the way one indicates a pew in a chapel one is no longer certain one believes in, and waited for her former student to sit down and become, for the duration of this conversation, the woman Vell had spent sixty years training her to be. The chair had been chosen for visitors. It was harder than the chair Vell sat in. This was not an accident. Vell had once explained the choice, in another conversation, by saying that visitors should sit in something that did not let them forget they were visiting. Mara had understood this at twenty and was only now beginning to understand what it cost Vell to have said it.

"You audited the roster." It was not a question.

Mara did not deny it. "A Keeper may review any redaction marker in the course of duties."

"May. And did you find what you were looking for, or only what you went to find."

Eleven classes where the charter named three. Mara let the number sit between them.

"The charter names three," Vell said at last, when the dome-light had moved a finger's width across the desk. "The settlement needed more. You were not yet born when the need became clear. A colony that remembers every grief becomes a colony that cannot build. We added, sparingly, and always with the return-clause intact. The founders, God bless them, had written the charter in the first flush of arrival, when the air still tasted of the ship and the dome still rang with the sound of every voice that had made it. They wrote as if memory were a thing one could afford to keep whole. They did not yet know that memory, kept whole, is a weight the bones cannot carry past the third generation. We learned. We added. We tried, where we could, to leave the doors unlatched behind us."

"Standing Order V-1. Sixty years pending review."

"Pending," Vell agreed, "because no Assembly has yet had the stomach to ratify what it already practices. Ratifying it would mean admitting the charter was insufficient. Admitting that is a different kind of instability. The colony has chosen to live the lie rather than speak it, and the lie has held the dome up for a hundred and forty years, and we have decided, together, without deciding, that holding the dome up is what we are for."

Mara heard the shape of it and almost forgave her for it, which was the most dangerous thing she could have felt in this room. "C-9," she said. "Joy in the lost. You classed a brother's laugh as destabilizing."

Something in Vell stilled. A deeper stillness than the Monthly Stillness. The kind a person makes when they have defended a thing too long to admit its shape, or when they are about to say something they have rehearsed so often it has stopped sounding like a defense and started sounding like weather.

"The classes were added to prevent rupture. After the transit fault, the one twelve years ago, the colony grieved in a way that threatened the looms. We broadened the Stillings. It held. The colony held. That is the only evidence I have ever needed."

"The transit fault," Mara said. The blank stone in the garden. The husband she did not remember weeping for. The space in the bed she had not understood was a space until she had audited the roster and seen his name, written in a hand she could almost recognize as hers, in a class she had signed without reading. "Was my husband's grief Stilled."

Vell looked at her directly then. "Yes."

One word. Vell had spent a career learning to say nothing in many sentences, and now she had put the colony's oldest wound into a single syllable and let it fall.

Mara had known, in the way one knows a healed gum, that something had been there once and was not there now. Hearing it named was different. The breath she had been holding for days found a new depth, and the depth was not relief, and it was not anger, and it was not even grief, because grief requires the memory of what was lost, and what was lost was exactly the thing that had been taken from her, which meant that the thing she was supposed to be grieving for she had been made unable to grieve for, which meant that the grief she felt was for the inability itself, which was a grief one could only describe as administrative, and which sat in her chest like a sealed docket. The docket had her name on it. The docket had been filed under her own hand. She could feel the seal in the bones of her sternum when she inhaled, the way one feels a set bone on a cold morning.

"Do not look deeper, Mara." Vell's voice had changed. For the first time in twenty years of knowing her, Mara heard something in it that sounded like pleading, though it was dressed, very carefully, as instruction. "The roster is maintained. The returns are possible. The colony is at peace. Some doors, once opened, do not close, and the wind that comes through them is not a wind the living can walk in."

Wind. There was no wind on Cessra. There had not been wind in a hundred and forty years. The colony was a sealed dome and the dome was held by atmosphere that did not move unless the pumps moved it, and the pumps moved it only when the sensors told them to, and the sensors were set to the founders' tolerances, and the founders had been born in places where wind meant weather, and weather meant uncertainty, and uncertainty had been the thing they had come up to escape. To speak of wind in the Elder's quarters was to speak in the old tongue, the tongue of the founder-settlers who had come up from Earth thinking they knew what atmosphere was, and Vell had used the word on purpose, and Mara heard her use it on purpose, and the hearing was a kind of love, which was also a kind of wound, because Vell had loved Mara, and Vell had Stilled the thing Mara loved, and these two facts were not contradictions but the same fact, repeated in different rooms of the same long house.

"Who decides which doors," Mara said, "if not the Keepers."

"Everyone decides by not deciding." Vell said it the way one says a thing one has said so often it has become the room itself. "That is the peace. You are a good Keeper. Be a good Keeper."

Mara stood. The chair did not scrape, because the floor had been laid to a tolerance that did not permit scraping, and Vell had never permitted scraping in her quarters, and the lack of scraping was now part of what the room meant, and the room was what Vell meant, and what Vell meant was what the colony meant, and what the colony meant was what Mara had meant, until four days ago, when she had opened a cache she had not known she carried and found in it a name that was her own and a class she had signed without reading. The class had been filed under V-1. The filing had been her own. The signature had been her own. The hand that signed it had been her own. These were not four facts but one fact, and the one fact was the door, and the door was what she was leaving through, and the leaving was the choosing, and the choosing was not yet a thing Vell had seen, though Vell had seen most things, and the seeing was what made Vell Vell, and Vell was now behind her, and the door was ahead, and the door did not close.

She left.

The hall was long and lit by the dome-light's slant, and she walked it without hurrying, because hurrying in the Keep would have been noticed and noticed was a kind of remembering she could not yet afford. The roster wall passed her on the left. Eleven lines. She did not touch it. Her hand, at her side, made a fist and then let go, and the letting go was its own small ceremony, and the ceremony was not yet in any class.

The garden was where it had always been, behind the Keep, in the air the pumps kept warm. The dome-light fell through it at the angle it always fell, and the ferns the founders had brought up in sealed tins from Earth grew along the path in the orderly way ferns grow when they are told they are a memorial and not a hedge. The ferns did not move. The dome-light did not flicker. The pumps hummed at the edge of hearing the way they had hummed for as long as Mara could remember, which was not as long as she had lived, which was the colony's longest joke on its Keepers and the one Vell had never laughed at. Mara walked the path. She let her eyes find the blank stone, the way they always found it now, in the way one learns to find a thing one has been told one has lost.

The blank stone was for her husband. She could not, now, remember him. Not the man, whom she could not surface. Not his name, which the love-that-cost class had taken. Not the way he had stood at the kitchen counter, or whatever counter he had stood at, because there had been a counter and a kitchen and she had not been wrong about there having been one, she had been wrong only about whose kitchen it was. The fact she could hold was the fact itself: that she had been made not to remember, which was a different and heavier thing than not remembering. Not-remembering was a wall in her own mind, and the wall had been built by a colleague she had respected, for a reason she could now name, and the reason had been the colony, and the colony had asked for the wall, and the colleague had built it, and Mara had signed the order, and the signing had been without reading, because she had trusted the colleague, and the trust had been the colony's, and the colony's trust was the Quiet, and the Quiet was the dome.

Vell had confirmed it. The taking of her own marriage, layered under three classes until the wife she had been was edited into a woman who had never been married. Acute grief, taken first, because the colony could not afford a Senior Keeper who walked the halls with the rawness of new loss in her eyes; the rawness would have reminded every other widow of the husband they had been made to forget, and the reminding would have been a kind of re-opening, and the re-opening would have undone the Stillings of the past twelve years, and the undoing would have cost the looms. Mourning-ritual, taken second, because the colony had decided, without deciding, that a public mourning by a public Keeper would have been a teaching, and the teaching would have been that mourning was permitted, and the permission would have been a precedent, and the precedent would have toppled the Quiet, and the Quiet was the dome. Love-that-cost, taken last, the deepest cut, the class that reached beneath the others and removed the root they had grown from, because the colony had finally understood that grief could be mourned away but love, un-Stillied, would keep returning love's shape to the mind, and the mind would keep reaching for the absent hand, and the reaching would be visible, and the visible would be a teaching. So love-that-cost had been Stillied at the root, and Mara had signed it, and her name was in the withholder field of the entry, and she had signed it without reading, and the not-reading was the Quiet, and the Quiet was her, and the her that signed was the her that had not been allowed to love, and the loving had happened anyway, and the happening had been taken, and the taking had been her own handiwork, and the handiwork was the book, and the book was the colony, and the colony was the dome.

She sat on the low wall.

The wall ran along the ferns. It was old, older than the Keep, older than the Standing Order, older than Vell. It had been built by a Keeper long retired, a Keeper Mara had read of in the histories and never met. The Keeper had built the wall for the garden's first generation, when the dead were still being remembered and the remembering was still permitted, and the wall had been a seat and a boundary and a place to set a cup of water for a name one had loved. The Keeper had retired before the redaction list had been expanded. The Keeper had not, in any class, been Stilled. The Keeper had simply gone on with the rest of her life, which was what one did when one had built a thing one was not ashamed of. Mara thought about this for a long time. The dome-light crossed the wall and found her hands. Her hands were her own. They had signed the order. They had signed it without reading, which was a different signing than signing with knowledge, but it was still signing, and a signature was a hand and a hand was a person and a person was the colony, and the colony had asked her to be the hand, and she had been the hand, and she was the hand now.

In the hidden cache she wrote. The cache was a private file in her personal docket, kept in the part of the docket the audit could not reach, kept there because some Senior Keepers kept such files, and the keeping was not against any class, and was not for any class, and was the kind of thing one did not speak of because speaking of it would have been its own class.

The wall was built by a Keeper I respected. For a reason I can name. It took my own marriage. I signed the order. I signed it without reading. I am not innocent. I am the wall's hand. The only repentance is to open what I closed.

It was a harsh sentence. She meant it. She had spent four days wanting to mean a softer sentence, and the softer sentence had not come, and the not-coming was its own evidence, and the evidence pointed at the door Vell had warned of, which was the door Vell had been warning of for sixty years, which was the door the colony had been warning of for a hundred and forty years, which was the door that did not close once it was opened, which was the door Mara had opened four days ago, when she had audited the roster.

A person who learns the combination of a lock they themselves helped set is still the person who helped set the lock. The book would not let her be a clean liberator. The book would not let her be a clean anything. The book was her, and the lock was her, and the combination was hers, and the unlocking, when it came, would be hers, and the cost of the unlocking would be hers, because the cost of anything in Cessra was borne by the person who had decided it could be borne, and the decision had been hers, and the bearing had begun.

She rose. The dome-light found her. She did not flinch from it.

Vell watched her go from the Elder's window. This Mara did not see, because she did not turn, and Vell did not call her back, because Vell had given the only thing she had left to give, which was a silence in which the younger woman could work, and the silence was the last mercy of a person who had helped build the wall, and the wall would fall, and the falling would be the book.

In the long transit tube back to the Keep, the commuters did not look up. Their eyes were on the floor, where the dome-light cast no shadow, because the floor was recessed and the light passed over it without landing. Mara had walked this tube a thousand times and never noticed the recess. Now she noticed it. The recess was a design choice. The design choice was a class. The class was the Quiet. The Quiet was the posture of a people who had been trained to look down, to keep their eyes on the work, to not meet the eye of the dead they had been made to forget. The commuters in the tube were her. The commuters in the tube were the colony. The commuters in the tube were the dome. The dome held. The dome would hold. The dome would hold until the day it did not, and the day it did not would be the day someone looked up.

She looked up.

The dome was above her, as it had always been. The transit tube was around her, as it had always been. The commuters were beside her, not looking. She looked at the dome and the dome did not look back, because the dome was a ceiling and not a person, and what she had been warned of was not the dome. What she had been warned of was the person who saw the dome and asked the dome what it was for, and the asking was a kind of opening, and the opening was the door Vell had named, and the door did not close, and the wind that came through was not a wind the living could walk in, and the living were all of them, and the wind was the Quiet asking itself whether it had been right, and the asking was the book, and the book was her, and she was walking in it.

She walked.

She did not walk faster. She did not walk slower. She walked the way a person walks who has chosen a door, and the choosing is not yet a thing that can be seen, and the walking is not yet a thing that can be stopped, and the door is behind her and ahead of her and inside her, and the inside is where the wall had been, and the wall is where the cache is, and the cache is where the sentence is, and the sentence is the harsh sentence, and the harsh sentence is the only honest sentence, and the honest sentence is the only repentance, and the repentance is the only opening, and the opening is the door, and the door is the book, and the book is the colony, and the colony is the dome, and the dome is the wind.

She reached the Keep. The dome-light found her at the door. She did not flinch from it. A person who has named the door does not fear the light that falls across it.

Chapter Six — What the Colony Forgot

Mara could have stopped.

Vell had warned her two days before, in the elder's room that smelled of lamp oil and old wool, and the warning had been dressed as instruction. Some doors, once opened, do not close. The wind that comes through them is not a wind the living can walk in. Mara had walked back to her quarters and stood at her desk and not opened the door. She had set her hand on the wood. She had looked at the grain. She had done what a Keeper does when a discrepancy appears in the record: she followed it to the root, because a Keeper who does not follow discrepancies is not a Keeper, only a sealer of lies.

The audit began on a Tuesday in the deep of the working week, when the Keep ran at its lowest hum. She requested, in her own name, structural access to the redaction markers across the colony's full history. Not the content of withheld memories. She could not reach the content without an order, and she was not yet willing to ask for an order, and the not-willingness was not cowardice, it was craft, because a Keeper who asks for content leaves a record of the asking, and a record of the asking is a thread a senior audit can follow. She asked, instead, for the skeleton: the count, the class, the timestamp, the authority of signature, the reason as recorded by the Keeper who had signed. The skeleton told the story the flesh was hiding. The flesh was the part the colony would notice if she took it. The skeleton no one had thought to lock, because no one had thought a Keeper would bother with bone when meat was forbidden.

The archive terminal she worked from sat in a quiet wing of the inner Keep, behind a door that no one else used after the third bell. The wing was not forbidden; it was simply forgotten, the way a room is forgotten when the people who once worked in it retire and the work is given to no successor. She had found the wing in her second year, when she was still young enough to walk the Keep end to end out of curiosity and still old enough to know which corridors a Senior Keeper could enter without comment. The door had no lock. The terminal inside it was an older model, with a hand-key and a flat amber screen, and the screen took two minutes to wake from cold. She let it wake. She let the wake be a kind of silence.

Going forward from the founders' year, she read the markers the way she had been trained to read a ledger: cleanly, without sympathy, holding the line of the entry in the eye the way a seamstress holds a seam. She let the structure speak first. She let the names wait. The first year's entries were seven. The second year's were eleven. The third year's were nine. She moved forward by fives, then by tens, and the numbers rose the way a tide rises, by a hand's breadth at a time, until you looked up and the sand was gone.

In the first forty years, the markers were few and charter-clean.

Grief. Rage. War-thought. The colony had been, by the founders' design, sparing; the ledger held, in some years, fewer than two hundred entries across the entire population of forty thousand. The founders had named three classes. Three classes, and the three were the ones a settlement on a moon needed to survive: the things that, if remembered, would have cracked the dome. The Charter of Subtraction said so plainly, in the language of the founders, which was a language of weights and edges and the careful naming of things that must be lifted before they could be set down. Mara read the Charter again, for the first time in years, and saw that the Charter had been written for a settlement that expected, within a decade, to return to Earth. The Charter had been written for a colony that intended to leave. The colony had stayed. The Charter had not been rewritten. The silence around the not-rewriting was its own document, and the document had begun to accumulate weight, and the weight had not been lifted by any hand, and the hand that did not lift it was, by inheritance, hers.

In year eighty, Vell signed Standing Order V-1.

The order was one page. The page authorised, on the grounds of post-fault stability, the introduction of additional classes and the standing exemption of those classes from Assembly review. Mara read it twice and set her hands flat on the desk. She had known the order existed. She had known, as every Senior Keeper knew, that V-1 had been pending review for sixty years. She had not known, until she pulled the markers, what the order had done in numbers.

The count jumped. Not a doubling. A multiplication. In the year of V-1 the ledger crossed from the low hundreds into the thousands and did not come back. The new classes appeared all at once, C-4 through C-11, and with them a rising tide of withholdings that the founders would not have recognised: mourning-rituals, dissent-memories, tenderness-under-duress, love-that-cost, joy-in-the-lost. The colony was not experiencing eight times the grief. It was being relieved of eight times the memory. Mara wrote the distinction in her own notebook, in pencil, in the small handwriting she used for things she was not yet ready to mean. The pencil was a small act of defiance against the terminal. The terminal remembered. The notebook did not. The notebook could be burned, and the burning would leave no trace in the lattice, and the lattice would not flag the absence of a thing it had never been told to keep.

By year one hundred the annual count had stabilised at a level eight times the founders' peak. The number held steady. It held the way a held breath holds: perfectly, and only because no one had let it out. Mara scrolled forward to year one hundred and forty and saw the same plateau, year on year, the curve flat as a floor. A colony that grieved less and less in fact, and remembered less and less in record, was a colony in which the two numbers had stopped speaking to each other. They had stopped speaking to each other sixty years ago. No one in the Keep had named the silence. The silence had been named the Quiet, and the Quiet had been named the peace, and the peace had been named the dome, and the dome had been named the home, and each name had been a paint laid over the same wall.

From the new withholdings she drew a sample, by hand, on every tenth class, the way a physician draws blood. The reasons were short. The reasons were, in the formal style of the ledger, sentences stripped to their joints. Mara read them in column, and the column read like a catalogue of a people's denied life.

C-6, tenderness under duress: citizen formed protective bond during transit accident; bond impaired return to duty. Withheld. Logged. Reversible.

C-5, dissent-memory: citizen recalled opposing dome-expansion vote; recall risked recurrence of opposition. Withheld. Logged. Reversible.

C-7, love-that-cost: citizen's attachment to a deported engineer reduced civic output by eleven percent. Withheld. Logged. Reversible.

Into the hidden cache she had been keeping for three weeks, she copied each entry into a column of her own, and the column lengthened until it was a portrait drawn in negatives: the citizen who loved a deported engineer and was relieved of the love so the looms would not suffer; the citizen who opposed a vote and was relieved of the opposition so the opposition would not recur; the citizen who mourned a child with a tree and was relieved of the mourning so the dead would not stay present; the citizen who laughed at a brother's joke across a meal and was relieved of the laugh so the joke could not recur. The phrase Logged. Reversible appeared at the foot of every entry. The phrase was a hinge left unrusted on a door that had not been opened in a hundred and forty years. Mara began to understand that the return-clause was not a protection. It was a sedative. It let the Keepers tell themselves they had not erased, only postponed, and postponement without end is erasure with a kinder name. The kinder name was what the Keepers taught the novitiates. The kinder name was what the novitiates taught the citizens. The kinder name was the Quiet. The Quiet was the dome. The dome was the home. The home was the kept thing. The kept thing was, in the end, the citizen.

Her hands had learned the keys by the second hour. By the third hour she had stopped telling herself she was not looking for her husband. She had looked for him since she sat down. She found him now.

His death was logged under C-1, acute grief. Year one hundred twenty-eight. The marker note read, in a clerk's hand she did not recognise: extended under C-4, prolonged mourning-ritual, Year 129. A second note, beneath, in the same hand: extended under C-7, love-that-cost, Year 130. Three classes, layered one upon the other, each extension signed by a Senior Keeper on standing order, each extension justified by the same four words: risk of recurrence. Mara read the three entries together and understood the engineering. The colony had not wanted a grieving Keeper. It had wanted a Keeper who had never been married. The colony had not merely eased her grief. It had rebuilt her, edited her synapse-map so that the loving itself was not retrievable, so that she would not, in some future crisis, be slowed by a dead man. The efficiency of it was its own horror. A people engineered not to be slowed by love is a people engineered not to be human, and the engineering had been signed, class by class, by Keepers who told themselves they kept the peace.

At the foot of the C-7 entry, in the withholder field, she read her own name.

Not Vell. Not a junior. Mara Els, Senior Keeper. She had, in her first years, signed the withholdings that erased her own marriage, under standing order, without ever reading what she signed. The healed gum had been her own handiwork. The hand had been hers. The hand had been young and obedient and had not known that obedience, repeated, becomes a kind of surgery performed on the self. Mara sat very still and let the knowledge settle, because the knowledge was not a surprise. It was a confirmation of what she had already felt in her own chest, in the blank stone in the garden, in the fact that she could not, when she tried, surface her husband's face. She could not surface the face because she had, with her own signature, ordered the surfacing forbidden. The order had not mentioned the face. The order had not needed to. The order had only mentioned the class. The class had done the rest. The class was a small word on a page, and the small word had eaten a marriage, and the marriage had been eaten by a hand that did not know what it was feeding.

Setting the entry aside, she returned to the sample. She was not yet finished.

What the structure showed next was worse than the count, because the count was a single number and the distribution was a shape, and the shape had an edge. The withholdings were not evenly spread across the colony. They fell, in their weight, along the seams of the work. The deep miners, who laboured in the dark and died young of dust and fault, had been Stilled at twice the rate of the dome-dwellers, because their grief was more frequent and more disruptive to the looms, and the colony had, in effect, priced grief by occupation, taking more from those who suffered more. Mara read the ratio and did not write it down, because some numbers, once written, become a kind of evidence a person cannot take back. She did not need to write it. The ratio wrote itself in her chest, in the small place where a Keeper keeps the things she has learned not to say aloud, and the place was not full, and the not-full was itself an indictment, because the place had been built for twice what it held and held, in fact, almost nothing at all.

Late in the shift, when the terminal's hum had grown familiar enough to be a kind of silence, she pulled a record from the mining sector. It was a miner's child. The child had been lost to a collapse in the second shaft. The death had been logged under C-1, acute grief, in the father's name, in the year one hundred and nineteen. A second entry, beneath, in a clerk's hand: extended under C-4, prolonged mourning-ritual, Year 119. A third: extended under C-7, love-for-the-child, Year 120. A fourth, in a different ink: extended under C-2, recurrence-of-name, Year 121. Four classes, layered, to ensure that the man who had lost a daughter could not surface her face. The fourth class had a name Mara did not recognise. She looked it up. C-2, recurrence-of-name. The class authorised the Stillings of any memory in which the deceased's name was spoken aloud. The colony had not only taken the father's grief. It had taken the name by which the grief could be named. The father could not, by the end, have said his daughter's name without the lattice flagging the syllable. The lattice had been taught to forget her for him. The teaching was in her own signature, in the signature of every Senior Keeper who had held the pen.

The four entries went into the cache, in order. She did not annotate them. They did not need annotation. They were, in their four short lines, the most complete indictment of the colony's cruelty dressed as care that she had yet read, and she had read, by then, several hundred. She sat with the entries for a long time, and the long time was not grief, exactly, because the capacity for the miner's grief was not hers to spend. The long time was the slow recognition that the lattice she had served for twenty-six years was not the instrument of care she had been taught to call it. The lattice was a net, and the net had been knitted, row by row, by hands like hers, and the hands had been told the knitting was mercy, and the mercy had been the name given to the knot.

Closing the terminal, she let the silence be the silence. The dome-light came through the high slit in the wall at the angle it always came, and the angle fell across the desk, and the desk held nothing on it now but her notebook and the small pencil. She did not cry. She had been Stilled of the capacity to cry for him years before she could have chosen otherwise. The Stilling had been thorough. The Stilling had been her own. She did not weep for the miner either. The capacity to weep for a stranger had been, in her, layered under so many classes that the weeping would have taken an order she was not yet ready to sign.

For the first time she understood the difference between peace and captivity.

The understanding arrived without a figure. It arrived the way the lattice's note arrived, as a fact the body already knew. The colony had taught her, for forty-four years, that peace was the absence of tremor in the surface of the pool. The colony had not taught her, because the colony had not known to teach her, that an absence of tremor maintained by force is not peace. An absence of tremor maintained by force is a held breath. A held breath held long enough becomes a posture. A posture held long enough becomes a skeleton. A skeleton held long enough becomes a wall. Mara had lived inside the wall. She had called it a dome. She had called it home. The dome was a wall. The home was a kept thing. She knew, in the small honest voice, which one she had been living.

She stayed in the archive until the third bell. She did not open the terminal again. She did not read the entries again. She let them rest in the cache the way a body rests before a funeral, in the care of someone who has agreed to do the carrying. She walked the long corridor back to her quarters slowly, because the corridor was the only place left in the Keep where a Senior Keeper could walk slowly without being asked why, and the asking would have required an answer she did not yet have in a shape she could speak. The dome-light followed her along the wall in its usual stripe, and the stripe passed across her hands as she walked, and her hands were steady, and the steadiness was a thing she had been taught to keep, and she was keeping it, and the keeping was, in its own small way, the last instruction of the order she had signed.

That night, in her quarters, she opened the hidden cache and added to it.

What she did not add was a confession, or a slogan, or a sentence about the colony, or Vell, or the wall. What she added was a name: her husband's. She added the three classes that had taken him, in the order in which they had been layered, with the years beside them. She added a single line beneath the three. The line was not a sentence about the colony. The line was a sentence about her.

I do not remember loving you. I am going to learn how.

The line was a promise to a ghost, and the ghost, being a ghost, could not hear it. Mara could hear it. The hearing was the first grief she had been allowed in twelve years, and it was small, and it was hers, and it was the seed of everything that followed. A Keeper who promises to a ghost instead of to the Assembly has already left the Quiet. The leaving is not loud. The leaving is the size of a pencil line in a hidden cache, written in the small handwriting of a person who has decided, against every teaching, to begin. The beginning is not a vow. A vow is loud. The beginning is a quiet notation in a ledger no one will read, made by a hand that knows the hand has been trained, and trained carefully, and that the training was the wall, and that the wall was also a kind of door, and that the door would have to be opened from inside.

Chapter Seven — The Keeper of What Was Taken

Mara did not tell Idris what she had found. She told no one. Instead, in the days after her first reading of the redaction class C-4, she did the only thing she knew how to do with knowledge she could not put down: she began to keep it.

What she kept was not the memories themselves. She could not yet reach them. The content of a withheld record was encrypted at the level of the lattice, and the keys to that encryption were held in the central looms, where her queries would be counted and weighed. What she could copy, without being seen to copy, was the structure around the memory: the class of the redaction, the date it was imposed, the name of the reviewing Keeper, the size of the file, the rubric under which it had been filed. The skeleton. The footprint a memory had left when it was taken. The shape of the hole, in other words, even if the thing that had been cut out of the hole was, for now, beyond her.

One by one, she copied these things into a private cache, kept on a personal drive she had never declared to the audit, and she called it, in the first week, nothing at all. A working folder. A holding pen for anomalies. She gave it a name only later, when she understood what kind of thing it was, and the name came to her the way names came to old Keepers of the central archive: from the structure she had copied most often, from the rubric that recurred in the lists like a root word in a language she was only beginning to read.

Under the colony's quiet measure, what she was doing was treason. A Keeper who keeps a record the Assembly has ordered forgotten is not a Keeper. She had no word for what she was becoming, and she did not try to find one. Words came later, when the thing itself had a shape. For now, she only worked.

Slow and dangerous, the work. Each copy left a faint query-trail in the system logs: a small heat signature, the kind a senior audit could surface with patience and the right prompts. She mapped the signatures she left by reading her own logs back to herself at the end of each session, the way a smuggler might walk her own path at low tide and count the footprints. She learned which kinds of copy left which kinds of trail. She learned which hours of the Stillness were busiest, and which were the stillest within the stillness. She learned to move inside the Monthly Stillness, when the lattice was already occupied with the routine withholdings of the month, and her small extractions hid in the noise of the larger work, the way a child's breath hides in the breath of a sleeping house.

In the third week she taught herself to copy only structure, never content, so that if she were discovered she could claim a procedural review: an audit of the audit, a Keeper checking her own records for inconsistency. The excuse held together. It held together because it was the kind of thing a senior Keeper would, in fact, sometimes do. She had done it herself, twice, in her thirties, on the orders of a previous Assembly. The pretense was not a lie in form; it was a lie only in intent. She had not yet lied to anyone. She was building, in case she ever had to lie, the architecture of a defensible lie: the scaffolding of a story that would bear her weight if the auditors ever came looking for her.

Patience in theft, she learned, was a different kind of patience. Not the patience of waiting but the patience of a thief learning a guard's patrol: the long study of a rhythm that was not her own, until she could place her own footfalls inside it without disturbing it. She had not imagined, in twenty years of service to the lattice, that she would learn this kind of patience. She had not imagined it was the kind she lacked.

During the small hours of the Stillness, she told herself a Keeper who steals from the record is still a Keeper, only inverted. Where she had once kept what the colony ordered kept, she now kept what the colony ordered forgotten. The distinction satisfied no one. Least of all her. But it let her continue. A sentence that lets you continue is not the same as a sentence that lets you rest, and she had not rested since the morning she had opened the file and read C-4 and decided, without deciding, to remember.

Week eight brought a threshold she had not expected. The hidden cache held, by then, the records of more than nine thousand withheld memories. Each record a few lines. Each line a name and a date and a class and a rubric. Each class a class of absence: a kind of thing the colony had decided it could not afford to remember, and so had agreed to forget together. She had not, when she began, expected the number to climb so fast. The lattice had its own appetite, and the appetite was larger than the colony's appetite for remembering. People were easier to forget than to recall. Whole practices of grief, whole vocabularies of repair, whole architectures of dissent, had been folded into the silence class by class over the decades, and the folders multiplied even when no one was watching, because the lattice itself, in the absence of a Keeper to prune it, would continue to file.

Alone in her workroom one night, she scrolled the list, and she felt something she had not felt since childhood.

Awe came first. That there were so many. That a colony of forty thousand people on a moon a hundred and forty years old had managed to forget so much, and to keep the forgetting so clean. The administrative beauty of it appalled her. Every redaction had a file. Every file had a keeper. Every keeper had signed. The colony's true history was written in the negative: a portrait of a people told in what had been taken from them, a portrait made of absences. And the negative went back, she saw, almost to the founding. Almost to the first hours.

Beneath the awe, a cold fury. Not the fury of a woman wronged but the slower fury of a woman who understood, at last, the scale of the work she had agreed to when she agreed to keep the record. Nine thousand files was not a number. It was a confession.

Among the nine thousand records, she chose one to copy in full. Not Cael's laugh, though the laugh was in the list, and her hand trembled as she scrolled past it. She chose a citizen's mourning-ritual, withheld under C-4, the ritual being the planting of a stone-tree for a daughter. A stone-tree, she read, was a small cairn shaped like a sapling, raised in the Garden of Small Stones by a parent who had lost a child. The colony had decided, sixty years ago, that this practice was destabilizing. The reasoning, recorded in the rubric, was that the planting kept the dead present in a way the Quiet could not absorb. The stone-tree, the rubric said, was a refusal of the colony's central promise: that a community which remembered together could survive together, and that a community which remembered the dead in the wrong way had already, in some small measure, begun to fall apart.

Three readings of the rubric. She had walked past a thousand stones in the Garden without knowing the stones were blank because the planting had been forbidden. She had assumed, in the way one assumes weather, that the Garden was simply old. That the stones had always been there, and would always be there, and that the silence of the Garden was the silence of a place which had outlived its grief.

That night, the Garden of Small Stones did not look like peace. She closed the cache and put on her coat and walked the path under the small amber lamps the colony set into the gravel. The stones were pale under the lamps. What she saw, with the new eyes her reading had given her, was a field of amputations: each stone a limb the colony had agreed to cut so it could keep walking, each blank surface a refusal that had been made collective, and therefore easy.

Near the path's end, one stone held her attention for a long time. She did not know whose daughter the stone had been for. She did not know whether the stone had ever been carved, or whether the carving had been the thing forbidden. She only knew that the stone was blank, and that the blankness was a choice, and that the choice had been made before she was born, and that she had walked past it ten thousand times without seeing the choice.

On her knees in the gravel, Mara put her hand on the cold surface of the stone. The stone was old enough to have lost its edges. It was smaller than she had expected. She had imagined, in the abstract, a monument. What she touched was the size of a sleeping child. A thing one parent could lift, if one parent were strong enough. A thing one parent could carry into a garden, in a year when carrying such things was still allowed, and set down in the gravel beside the path, and weep over, and come back to. The fact of the size made the absence worse. Whatever had been carved here had not been large. It had been a name, perhaps. A single date. The kind of mark that a colony of forty thousand people could have carried, easily, in the silence of its own small griefs. The colony had decided it could not. Mara put her forehead against the stone and made a promise that did not yet have words, only the shape of words, only the weight of a vow taken in the dark by a woman who had not yet understood what she was vowing to.

Back in her workroom, with her coat off, the words came. That night, in the private cache, she wrote the sentence that named her:

I am the Keeper of what was taken. The colony has a memory of record. I will keep a memory of omission. If the two are ever allowed to meet, the colony may choose. Until then, I choose to remember that we forgot.

Read back to herself, the sentence was complete. She did not amend it. She did not add to it. A naming sentence, she understood, was a thing one wrote once, and then lived into.

It was the first act of a self she had not met. The Quiet, in her, was no longer a held breath, which was what she had been taught it was, what every child in the colony was taught it was: the gentle pressure the community exerted on the inner weather, so that no one person's storm could become the weather of all. The Quiet, in her, had become a wall. She had stepped outside of it. The wind Vell had warned of, the wind that came when the wall was no longer a wall, was real, and it was cold, and it was the only air she had ever truly breathed.

Whether the cache was a way of preserving what had been taken, or a way of forcing what had been taken to be returned, or a way of simply outliving the colony's right to forget, she did not yet know. She only knew she could not stop. Each week she copied a little more. Each week the cache grew by a thousand records, then by twelve hundred, then by fifteen hundred, as the lattice's appetite for forgetting continued to outpace her hand. The lattice had not stopped filling the silence class since the colony's founding, and would not stop now, regardless of whether one senior Keeper in one small workroom was watching.

There was more to keep than she had feared. There was more to keep, by the end of week eight, than she could read in a lifetime. She read it anyway, slowly, the way one reads a long letter from someone one has not heard from in years. The structures told her things the colony had never told her. The structures told her that an entire genre of lullaby had once existed for children whose births were difficult, and that the genre had been filed under C-3 in her own mother's lifetime. The structures told her that the colony had, for thirty years, maintained a public ledger of debts of gratitude between families, and that the ledger had been folded into the silence in the year after the second dome-collapse, when the colony had decided, in a single Assembly session, that gratitude between families was a destabilizing inheritance. The structures did not tell her why. They told her only that the files existed, and that the files had keepers, and that the keepers had signed.

Not a return. Not yet. What she was building, she understood, was an archive of the crime, a record that the taking had happened: so that if a future Assembly ever grew the stomach to order a return, the memory of what had been taken would be there to return to. She called it, in her private notes, the Understory: the layer beneath the visible forest, the part of the wood that did the holding without being seen. The Understory was her answer to the wall. So that the colony, someday, might have the choice its parents had decided it could not be trusted with.

No Assembly she could yet imagine unseated appeared in her plans. She did not yet know she would be the one to force the choice. She did not yet know that the patience she was learning would carry her into a room she could not yet picture, in front of an Assembly she could not yet see, and that she would set the cache down on the table between them and wait. She only knew she would spend the rest of her life making sure the taking was, at last, a thing someone could point to and name.

A quiet war, the cache. Fought not with weapons but with copies. Not with shouts but with a Keeper's thumb, steady in the Stillness, authorizing what the Assembly had forbidden her to authorize. The thumb, once used, would not unauthorize. The cache would grow until it held a colony's denied life. And the holding would become, in time, the giving.

By the end of the eighth week she understood that the war had been going on for a long time before she had joined it. Other Keepers, in other decades, must have felt what she was feeling, must have begun their own quiet extractions, must have carried their own small caches in their own small workrooms, and must have died, or been reassigned, or simply grown tired. The colony had survived their quiet wars the way a forest survives a season of slow rot: by continuing to look green from a distance. Somewhere, perhaps, in some unindexed corner of the lattice, the ghosts of those caches were still ticking over, fragment by fragment, in drives no one remembered to retire.

Of the door itself, she could not yet see a thing. She could see only the thumb, and the screen, and the long list of names she was writing into a forest no one else could see.

Secrecy was not the discipline the work had taught her, by the end of the eighth week. She had always been good at keeping her own counsel. The discipline she had lacked was the patience of a person who is building something for a future she will not live to see, and who must therefore build it as if the future were already here, and she were only catching up to it.

How the two memories would meet, she did not yet know: the memory of record and the memory of omission. She only knew she would spend the rest of her life making sure they could.

Chapter Eight — The First Return

She wanted, with a hunger that surprised her, to know what a withheld memory felt like from the inside. Not the scar in the lattice, not the marker, not the empty footprint in the directory. The thing itself: a life someone had lived and lost.

The structure she had copied into the hidden cache told her where nine thousand absences used to live. It did not tell her what any of those absences had been. A catalogue of rooms with the doors removed, every handle filed down to a circle of polished wood. She could walk the corridors of the colony's grief and never once hear what the grieving had sounded like when it was still allowed a sound.

She had come to the console wanting to understand the grammar of an absence. The lattice, in the technical sense it used, classed that kind of understanding as theft. A Keeper could audit the shape of a withholding. A Keeper could not, without an order of the Assembly, open the thing a withholding was withholding.

A hundred and forty years ago a colony had learned, the hard way, what unchecked remembering could do to a closed ecology of forty thousand people pressed against the inside of a glass bubble on a moon with no second chance. The colony had learned by losing two of its eight overflow chambers in a single decade, one to a grief they could not stop, one to a joy they could not bound. It had learned by watching a five-year-old go quiet in a way no machinery could mend. And it had done, given those lessons, what closed ecologies do. It had built a lattice that could re-name things, and re-named redaction Stilling, and re-named the lattice Keepers, and re-named Keepers servants of the colony's peace, and by the time Mara had been born into the structure, the re-naming had been so thorough that what her office actually did and what her office said it did had the same weight, and the colony slept.

A console is a private country. A senior Keeper's reads were not audited as thefts but as duties; whatever she summoned here, in the humming blue dim of her office, was assumed to have been summoned on behalf of the body of Keepers, which was in turn the conscience of the colony. She had used that presumption for years, unthinkingly, the way a person uses floorboards. Tonight, for the first time, she went looking for the gap beneath them.

She knew which absence she wanted.

Cael Vann, class C-9, joy in the lost. The laugh that had been redacted from his brother's record nine years ago and described, in the Understory's short note, as tenderness under duress. The phrase had annoyed her the first time she read it. Tenderness. Cael Vann's laugh had been named, by the record, the way a man names a wound, because joy in this colony was a kind of wound, the kind that bled inward and refused to clot. The Assembly had Stilled it not because joy was wrong but because joy was unreliable, and the colony could not afford unreliable things.

Thirty-one he had been. A loom engineer like his brother, and the looms ran on quiet the way blood runs on iron, and a man who threw back his head and laughed in a loom-bank was a man who briefly broke the quiet, and the quiet healed over him the way a tide heals over a footprint, and the next shift did not even know he had been there.

She queried the deep store for the content associated with Cael's redaction marker, not to return it to Idris but to read it herself. The lattice answered in the flat ceremonial voice it used for Keeper transactions: Content associated with marker C-9-131-A: held. Access requires order of Assembly or Keeper-Elder override. You hold Keeper-Elder override.

Vell had granted her the override when she named her Senior Keeper. Vell, who had once been a young Keeper on the third shift and was now the voice of the body itself, had pressed a key and made her able to do anything the Keepers could do. She had never used it. Using it, her predecessor had warned her in her first week, was the kind of thing one did not do unless one was sure of what one was doing. She had been sure of nothing for nine years.

She was sure of something now.

The memory opened like a door into a room she had not known was there.

Joy, the memory insisted, was not a sound. It was a posture. Cael Vann stood in the loom-bank with his head thrown back, his hands lifted off the core housing to show he was not touching anything that mattered, and his whole body was the laugh; his throat, his shoulders, the small helpless shake of his rib-cage that meant a man had stopped being able to stop. Beside him, Idris, younger than the Idris she had spoken to that morning in the corridor, the Idris whose gum had healed over a laugh he had never been allowed to lose, was laughing too. The laugh of the younger brother was the laugh of the older brother reproduced almost exactly, two engines tuned to the same misfiring. A trainee stood behind them with her hands on the bank housing, mortified, the source of the trouble, and Cael was laughing at her too, not unkindly but in the helpless way a person laughs at a small perfect absurdity.

The absurdity was a mis-set core. A trainee had keyed it one click off spec, and the bank had stuttered, and the stutter, replayed through the loom's feedback, had for sixteen seconds produced a sound no one in the colony had heard before: a chord that was almost, but not quite, an octave of something that ought not to exist. Cael had heard it. Cael had heard that it was funny. Cael had laughed. The memory carried the laugh in full, not as a recording but as a reliving, every breath and catch of breath intact, and the reliving moved through Mara the way a song moves through a person who has forgotten they knew it.

She had never met Cael Vann. She had no brother at all; she had been an only child, and her husband had been an only child, and their daughter was an only child, and the small family she had made by losing people had left her alone with no practice at the particular kinship of brothers laughing together at a machine's mistake. Yet the laugh moved through her as if it were hers. Because joy is contagious, and the colony had been trying for a hundred and forty years to make it stop being contagious, and the colony had been wrong, and the wrongness was a thing her body could feel in the dark of her office on a night when no one was watching.

She sat a long time after the laugh ended, in the humming console-light, with her hands flat on the desk and her eyes unfocused on the bank of read-outs that had already moved on to other business. She was laughing, she realized. Quietly, alone, in the dark office of her senior Keeping, for a dead man she had never known, and the laugh was a small unreasonable thing moving through her the way a memory moves through her when it has been permitted to remain a memory. It had been nine years since she had laughed in a way her body registered as laughter. It had been longer.

There were three things she could do with what she had found. She could leave it where it was, un-read by any human, and let the deep store hold it as it had always held it, a dormant artifact in the sealed archive: this was the safest course, the one most exactly aligned with her oath, and the one she had already failed by the act of reading. She could pretend the reading had not happened. She could return the memory to Idris, but to surface the C-9 class to the Assembly was, in the current political weather, to set a torch to her own position and possibly to the Understory she had been quietly assembling for nine years: the Assembly would know within an hour, and a brother so loved that his laugh had been classified as tenderness under duress was a brother they would have to explain to the colony, and the colony would have to be told why they had not been allowed to miss him. Or she could copy it. Move the memory whole and intact into the hidden cache, take the door into the room and put it in a place where only she could open it, let Idris continue for the time being to live with his healed gum, let the colony continue to sleep its good Stilled sleep, and let herself be the only soul in Cessra who knew that Cael Vann had laughed the way her husband once had laughed, helplessly, at something stupid and true.

The second option was the honest one and the kindest, and it would kill her.

She closed the file, copied the full contents into the hidden partition she had spent nine years building behind the structure, the loom-bank and the mis-set core and the trainee's mortified face and the thrown-back head of Cael and Idris's younger laugh answering his brother's older one and the sixteen-second chord that had almost been an octave of something impossible. She typed her own key sequence to seal it. She typed a second sequence to make it invisible to routine audits. She sat back in the chair.

Below her office window the looms sang in their tuned silence. The colony hummed its calm note, which was no note at all, which was the absence of the notes the colony had agreed not to sing. Somewhere on the lower terraces Idris Vann was asleep, or sitting at his kitchen table over a cup of something, and his gum was whole, and he did not know he had ever had an older brother whose body had been a laugh, and tomorrow he would say again, I had a brother, Cael, dead nine years, I don't like to dwell, and Mara would nod, and the lying would be a small quiet thing inside her, no louder than the colony's own quiet.

Now the Understory was no longer an inventory. For the first time it held not the absence of a memory but the memory itself, alive, separable, returnable, a thing a body had lived and lost and that a body, given leave, could live again.

She held a brother's laugh against a colony that had decided he must not be carried. Tenderness under duress. The carry was supposed to be the colony's, distributed evenly, held in common. The colony had decided, in some past crisis, that a thing carried collectively was a thing carried safely. The colony had also decided, in that same past crisis, that a thing too heavy for one person to hold alone was a thing one person must not hold alone. Both decisions were reasonable. Both decisions were what had produced her office, the console, the override, the quiet hum of the loom below her. She did not yet know, sitting in her chair with the key-sequence fading from her fingertips, that neither decision had ever been tested against a person who had actually held one of the lost things and been quietly undone by it.

The shape of what she had committed to began to clarify itself to her in copying that laugh into the dark of her private store. Not whether. That was settled, settled by the body of her sitting in the chair, settled by the throat-muscles that had remembered how to laugh without consulting her first. She would return what had been taken. The how was the rest of her life.

How to pour a hundred and forty years of stolen joy and stolen grief back into a people who had forgotten they were missing it without drowning them in the flood. How to begin slowly enough that no one broke. How to begin at all, when the beginning itself was a thing the colony had been trained to mis-name as theft.

She set the problem down like a piece of work on a workbench. It would be there in the morning. The morning would be there too. The colony's longest night, in its longest year of forgetting, had ended at some point during the last hour without her noticing, and somewhere on the lower terraces a shift was coming on and another going off and the loom-bank was running in its tuned way and a forty-year-old man she had spoken to that morning was brushing his teeth over a sink, the healed gum of him ordinary to himself, the brother of him sealed over like a tide-pool, and the memory of Cael was a closed room in her console with the door standing open and the air beginning, at last, to move.

She did not sleep well. She did not sleep at all, for a long time.

What came instead of sleep, when it came, was not the Quiet. The Quiet had been the colony's gift to its exhausted Keepers for a hundred and forty years: a small legal dose of the same stillness the lattice applied to the public, the mind folded gently around its own clean absence of itself. Mara had taken it every night for twenty-two years. She had not taken it tonight, because the laugh was still in her, still moving through her, and she did not want to fold anything gently around it. She wanted to let it move. She wanted to see what would happen if she let it move.

The thing that happened was a sleep she did not usually have, and with it a dream she did not usually have.

In the dream the door had no handle. It was just an opening in a wall she had walked past for nine years without seeing, and on the other side of the opening there was a wind, and the wind was carrying things she could not quite see, small sounds and small shapes and the edges of people turning toward her. The wind was not cold. It was not warm. It was what air was in a room that had been closed a long time and was at last a room with the door standing open, the door letting in whatever air was out there, which was the rest of what the room had been missing.

She was not afraid.

She was, in the dream, no longer the only one at the door. There was no one else, actually; she was alone in the room, and the door was open, and she was standing just inside it, with her hand on the frame, and the wind was the colony and the colony was the wind and neither of them knew yet what was coming through.

When she woke the console was humming and the room was ordinary and the laugh was still a small unreasonable thing in her chest, moving when she breathed.

Chapter Nine: The Lower Archive

The lower archive waited beneath the Keep, below the looms and the humming galleries where Cessra made its present tense. Mara had not descended there in twenty years. She remembered the stair as a service passage, narrow and unfinished, with conduit pipes showing through the walls and a door whose lock had never been connected to the lattice. Since her first year as a Keeper, she had thought of the place as a tomb. The upper Keep held the colony's sanctioned memory, polished and indexed, its omissions made smooth. Down here lay what remained before the charter learned to call itself permanent.

An audit of the deep store brought her back. The name appeared in a marginal note attached to a paper census, written in a hand that predated the digital ledger and its clean categories. Soren Taal, it said. C-5 dissent-memory, withheld, Year 95. Citizen subsequently unpersoned by neighbors' sync. The marker had not been entered into the ordinary stream because the paper had never been translated into lattice form. Its warning had survived as ink, a small dark refusal in the margin of a record meant to count bodies and provisions.

Cold met her first at the bottom of the stairs. It passed through the fabric of her uniform and touched the bones of her hands. Paper gave the rooms their other quality. The smell rose from shelves and boxes, dry and faintly sweet, mingled with dust, old glue, iron, and the mineral chill of stone. Beneath those scents lay the particular silence of untouched things, a stillness without the lattice's pressure. Nothing here listened for an authorized version. Nothing anticipated correction. The archive had been left alone so long that its neglect had become a kind of preservation.

At the end of the passage, a single lamp burned over a desk. Its yellow circle contained a man bent above an open book. He was broad through the shoulders, though age had drawn him inward, and his hair had gone mostly silver. When he looked up, Mara saw a lined face and eyes that held their attention without offering it to any system. She had spent thirty years among people whose expressions softened as soon as the lattice noticed them. This man's gaze did not soften. It rested on her with the wary steadiness of someone accustomed to keeping one thing safe at a time.

He closed the book with one hand. The cover made a low, final sound against the desk. “You are the Keeper,” he said. His voice was quiet, but the room carried it. “I wondered when one of you would come down. The markers are bleeding, are they not?”

“You know about the markers?” Mara asked.

“Nothing stays buried in a place built of paper.” His eyes moved to the badge at her throat, then returned to her face. “Not if someone keeps reading.”

“I found your name.” She removed the copied note from her pocket, though she did not yet unfold it. “I do not know what ‘unpersoned by neighbors' sync’ means.”

Soren Taal regarded her for a long moment. No lattice prompt appeared between them to suggest a gentler formulation. No social cue arrived to tell her whether she had crossed a boundary. He seemed to take the sentence as it stood, an object set on the desk between them.

“Then you really do not know,” he said. “That is almost a mercy.”

The explanation began with the ordinary word sync. The colony synchronized its citizens constantly, smoothing local discrepancies so that a shared event could remain shared. If a memory was Stilled from one person, the lattice searched the surrounding memories for connected traces. It adjusted a spouse's account, a neighbor's recollection, a child's association, until the removed thing no longer cast a visible shadow. The work was described as compassionate. No citizen should be left carrying a contradiction alone.

Soren spoke without haste, and each sentence seemed to lose heat as it entered the room. “If the person is Stilled often enough, the adjustment goes further. Dissent, love, grief, the parts that keep returning, they become difficult for everyone nearby to locate. The neighbors do not remember forgetting. They stop remembering the meeting itself. They stop having met the person.”

Mara unfolded the note. The handwriting looked suddenly cruel in its restraint. “Wren,” she said. “That was the name attached to your record.”

“My partner.” Soren's hand settled on the closed book. “Wren opposed the dome expansion. She was Stilled for C-5. When her opposition surfaced in me, the lattice Stilled her again through the memories I carried. Then it reached the people who knew us. Conversations, meals, work shifts, the small arrangements by which one life touches another, all of it was reconciled. By the time I came here, half the colony had no record of ever knowing her.”

The word unpersoned had not appeared in the charter. Mara searched the clauses she knew by heart and found no language that admitted such an outcome. The charter named correction, continuity, and the protection of communal balance. It did not name a woman being removed from the history of every person who had loved her. The result hung before Mara like the shadow of a wall, not part of the structure, yet cast by it all the same.

Soren reached beneath the lamp and drew a large volume from the stack beside him. “This is the pre-charter census. The founders kept it during the first five years, before the lattice was whole. Names, dwellings, work assignments, disputes. They wrote down what could not yet be mirrored.”

Paper rasped against paper as he opened the book. The pages were the color of old bone, their edges darkened by years of handling. Columns ran across them in careful ink. Some entries had been amended, some crossed through, some annotated by a second hand. The marks possessed no seamlessness. A correction remained visible as a correction. An uncertain figure did not become certainty merely because a later clerk preferred it that way.

Wren's entry lay several pages in. Her name was written plainly. Citizen. Residential ring three. Mechanical survey. Dissenting on dome expansion, Year 94. Beside the line, someone had fastened a small photograph with a strip of yellowed adhesive. The woman in it had turned slightly away from the camera. She was laughing at something outside the frame, one hand lifted as if to fend off the person who had caught her unguarded.

Her eyes were Soren's eyes. That resemblance made the photograph more intimate than any portrait could have been. Mara felt the familiar instinct to inventory the image, to identify its date, its source, its evidentiary grade. The impulse faltered when she saw the shape of the laugh. It reminded her of Cael's laugh, before the Keep had taught her to hear it as an irregularity. The colony had decided that certain sounds must not be carried, and then had made the silence seem natural.

For several breaths, Mara could not look away. The photograph contained no argument, no official accusation, no record of the dome debate. It held a person's face in the instant before she turned back toward the world. That small freedom had survived where the more legible facts had not. Soren watched Mara read the image, and his silence asked for nothing. It was the first unforced courtesy she had encountered that day.

“I am building a record,” Mara said. The confession rose before she had chosen to make it. “Of what was taken. Structure, and some content. It is outside the audit stream.”

“It is treason,” she added.

A smile moved across Soren's face. Mara had seen smiles in the upper Keep, but they were usually acknowledgments, brief social permissions granted and withdrawn. This one began in the eyes. It made him look younger, and because it was not managed, it seemed almost dangerous.

“Then you are the first Keeper in my life who was not a sealer of lies,” he said. “Sit. I will show you the rest of the tomb. It is fuller than you think.”

They worked through the day by the pool of the single lamp. Soren brought out boxes one at a time, never opening a second until he had returned the first to its place. He did not treat the papers as relics. He handled them with the exact care of a person who knew that reverence could become another form of distance. There were names of citizens whose dissent had vanished from the upper records, dates of mourning rituals banned after the charter, and letters between people whose love had later been revised into an administrative misunderstanding.

Among the bundles were reports from the colony's first years, when disagreements had still been written down instead of harmonized away.
